```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models. Even classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.93$ produce over 50\% downstream error risk.
5	
6	The consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\% of confusion matrices cause \emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\% (13.7\% violation rate at diagonal $\geq 0.7$, comprising 2.1\% sign reversal and 11.7\% amplification).
7	
8	Why does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry between binary and multi-class annotation error has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.
9	
10	Compounding this gap, \citet{kim_garg2025} show that LLM errors are highly correlated across models (60\% agreement when both err), undermining the common assumption that ensembling multiple LLMs can mitigate annotation error. This finding strengthens the case that analyzing the \emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.
11	
12	We address these gaps with three contributions:
13	
14	\begin{enumerate}
15	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven) ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.
16	
17	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.
18	
19	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL, MC-SIMEX, PPI++) across all seven topologies and show that correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent. A Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result proves that matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies.
20	\end{enumerate}
21	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}
5	The epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\cite{gustafson2004}. \citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. Our work extends this line by providing a systematic taxonomy across seven DAG topologies with closed-form expressions for the $K$-class case, identifying which topologies preserve and which break the sign-flip guarantee.
6	
7	\paragraph{Text classifier errors in causal studies.}
8	\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error, noting matrix adjustment correction but without formal bias theorems. \citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \citet{yang_adomavicius2018} formalized the two-stage pipeline problem (data mining followed by econometric analysis) and proved that ML-generated variables systematically bias downstream regression. \citet{keith_jensen_oconnor2020} surveyed the text-and-causal-inference landscape, identifying classifier measurement error as a key open challenge. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers on clinical data but covered only one topology (unobserved confounding). \citet{chen_bhattacharya_keith2024} used zero-shot NLP models as proxies in proximal causal inference, proving identification conditions for one DAG structure. We generalize across all seven topologies with multi-class confusion matrices, providing both bias expressions and sensitivity analysis tools absent from prior work.
9	
10	\paragraph{LLM annotation quality.}
11	Recent work has quantified the downstream risks of LLM annotation. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrated across 37 tasks that 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations. \citet{kim_garg2025} showed that LLM errors are 60\% correlated across models, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. \citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance via linear mixed models but explicitly excluded causal identification. \citet{stolwijk_boukes_trilling2025} found that LLM annotations differ from human annotations in prevalence even when per-sample accuracy is adequate, and \citet{cheng_mayya_sedoc2024} decomposed annotation measurement error into four source types. These works diagnose the annotation quality problem; our contribution provides the causal-analytic bridge connecting annotation error structure to downstream causal estimate validity.
12	
13	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}
14	Our ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures, and \citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds. \citet{vanderweele_li2019} extended sensitivity analysis to differential measurement error but only for binary settings. \citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy (``how much bias would be needed to explain away the finding?'') to annotation-induced bias, with three complementary measures tailored to different topology-dependent failure modes.
15	
16	\paragraph{Correction methods.}
17	Prediction-powered inference~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are complementary to our framework: our topology-dependent ASV analysis identifies \emph{when} correction is needed and \emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \emph{how}.
18	
```

Now let me evaluate the Introduction and Related Work for narrative improvement opportunities, as the Director asked. Let me think about what could be strengthened without requiring new experimental data.

**Introduction Assessment:**

Strengths:
1. Good opening hook with the 31% and 50% statistics from Baumann et al.
2. Clear articulation of the "why multi-class is different" argument (paragraph 3)
3. Well-structured three contributions

Weaknesses / Improvement opportunities:
1. **Motivation framing**: The opening focuses on LLM annotation quality but doesn't frame the *causal inference* angle as strongly. 

The core insight here is that measurement error and causal DAG structure are deeply intertwined — this connection needs to be front and center from the start rather than buried later.

2. **"Why now" argument is weak**: The introduction doesn't explain why this problem is urgent *now*. It could be strengthened by noting: (a) the scale of LLM adoption in computational social science, (b) the trend of using multi-class labels (not just binary) — sentiment analysis with 5 categories, stance detection with 3-4 categories, etc., (c) the gap between ML evaluation (F1) and causal validity.

3. **Contribution 3 phrasing**: "Topology-aware correction guidance" is good but could be framed more sharply to emphasize that the DAG structure itself determines which corrections are valid.

4. **Missing "big picture" framing**: The paper could benefit from a sentence about what this means for the broader field — e.g., "our results suggest that the standard practice of evaluating LLM annotators by F1 alone is insufficient for causal inference applications; topology-specific sensitivity analysis is necessary."

5. **Paragraph 4 (Kim & Garg)**: This paragraph feels like an afterthought. It could be better integrated into the motivation.

**Related Work Assessment:**

Strengths:
1. Well-organized by topic (5 paragraphs)
2. Good positioning — clearly states what each prior work does and what gap remains
3. Good "bridge" function — connects measurement error, NLP, LLM quality, sensitivity analysis, and correction methods

Now I'm looking at the weaknesses in the related work section — there are some gaps in how certain areas are covered and how they connect back to the main argument. The related work section also misses the broader context around AI-assisted research and automated social science, which would strengthen the overall motivation. Let me verify the AAAI template settings — checking if `\textsc` is supported and confirming that section numbering is properly suppressed — then I'll report my findings to the Director.

Figure 6（ASV heatmap）→ Appendix B\n\n**主正文保留**：\n- Figure 1（DAG taxonomy）、Figure 2（bias distribution）、Figure 3（ASV ranking）— 核心结果\n- Figure 4（F1 vs bias scatter，原 fig5）— 关键实证发现\n- Table 1（taxonomy summary）、Table 2（ASV grid）— 紧凑核心数据\n\n**文本压缩**：\n- §4.4 N-sensitivity：2 段 → 1 段\n- §4.5 Binarization Collapse：2 段 → 1 句 + appendix 引用\n- §4.6 Empirical Validation：删除\"Scope and ongoing work\"段（conclusion limitations 已覆盖）、压缩\"Confusion matrix structure\"段\n- §4.7 Correction Guidance：6 段 + Table + Algorithm → 3 短段 + appendix 引用\n- Conclusion：Limitations 从 4 段压缩为 1 段，Future work 从 4 条压缩为 1 句\n\n**编译状态**：PASS，0 critical warnings，0 undefined references\n\n## Introduction & Related Work 叙事评估\n\n**Introduction 提分空间**：\n1. **\"Why now\" 论证偏弱**：开头聚焦 LLM annotation quality，但没有强调 multi-class 在社科中的普遍性（stance detection K=3-4, sentiment K=5, topic K=10+）。可加一句量化 multi-class 的普遍程度\n2. **Contribution 3 的 negative result 不够醒目**：FWL invariance 证明 matrix-based corrections 对 4 个 topology 完全无效——这是一个 surprising 结果，但目前措辞不够有冲击力。建议改为先陈述 negative result 再给 positive result\n3. **F1 ≠ causal validity 的核心洞察埋在 contribution 1 里**：这是论文最有实践影响力的发现（fig5 的核心 message），建议在 Introduction 第 1-2 段就预告：标准 F1 评估对因果推断不充分\n4. **Kim & Garg 段落整合不紧凑**：第 4 段像附加物，可融入第 1 段的 motivation 中\n\n**Related Work 提分空间**：\n1. **缺乏显式 gap statement**：每段末尾是泛泛的 \"our work extends/generalizes\"，可改为每段明确指出\"X 没做什么、这正是我们框架解决的\"\n2. **ASV vs E-value 对比深度不够**：当前只说 \"adapts the philosophy\"，可加一句解释核心差异：E-value 量化 unmeasured confounding 的强度，ASV 量化 annotation error 到达危险阈值所需的 δ 值\n3. **Text classifier + LLM annotation quality 可合并**：这两段有重叠，合并后可腾出空间给 gap statement\n\n**总结**：这些改进不需要新实验数据，纯叙事层面的重构。预计 Introduction 改进可提升 reviewer 的 motivation 和 novelty 评分。是否需要我现在执行这些改动？",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CukMseduJrJjYhagvaigmo`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models. Even classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.93$ produce over 50\% downstream error risk.
5	
6	The consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\% of confusion matrices cause \emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\% (13.7\% violation rate at diagonal $\geq 0.7$, comprising 2.1\% sign reversal and 11.7\% amplification).
7	
8	Why does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry between binary and multi-class annotation error has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.
9	
10	Compounding this gap, \citet{kim_garg2025} show that LLM errors are highly correlated across models (60\% agreement when both err), undermining the common assumption that ensembling multiple LLMs can mitigate annotation error. This finding strengthens the case that analyzing the \emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.
11	
12	We address these gaps with three contributions:
13	
14	\begin{enumerate}
15	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven) ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.
16	
17	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.
18	
19	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL, MC-SIMEX, PPI++) across all seven topologies and show that correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent. A Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result proves that matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies.
20	\end{enumerate}
21	
```

  "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models---and these errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err)~\\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nThe consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\\% of confusion matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\\% (13.7\\% violation rate at diagonal $\\geq 0.7$). Moreover, standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies---from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%---demonstrating that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.\n\nWhy does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \\geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \\emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a surprising negative result: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance theorem proves that matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies---the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\\end{enumerate}\n"

This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \\emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a surprising negative result: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance theorem proves that matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies---the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models. Even classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.93$ produce over 50\\% downstream error risk.\n\nThe consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\\% of confusion matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\\% (13.7\\% violation rate at diagonal $\\geq 0.7$, comprising 2.1\\% sign reversal and 11.7\\% amplification).\n\nWhy does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \\geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry between binary and multi-class annotation error has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.\n\nCompounding this gap, \\citet{kim_garg2025} show that LLM errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err), undermining the common assumption that ensembling multiple LLMs can mitigate annotation error. This finding strengthens the case that analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven) ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \\emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL, MC-SIMEX, PPI++) across all seven topologies and show that correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent. A Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result proves that matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\\cite{gustafson2004}. \\citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \\citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \\citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. However, none of these works systematically characterize how bias varies \\emph{across} DAG topologies for a given confusion matrix. Our taxonomy fills this gap: given the same $K$-class confusion matrix, we derive how bias direction and magnitude differ across seven canonical topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}\n\\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models, and \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but explicitly excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework bridges this gap: we provide the causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}\nOur ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures: the minimum strength of unmeasured confounding needed to explain away an observed association. \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy to a different bias source: rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification $\\delta$ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold---with three complementary measures tailored to topology-dependent failure modes (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction methods.}\nPrediction-powered inference~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.\n"
}

Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy to a different bias source: rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification $\\delta$ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold---with three complementary measures tailored to topology-dependent failure modes (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction methods.}\nPrediction-powered inference~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\\cite{gustafson2004}. \\citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \\citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \\citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. Our work extends this line by providing a systematic taxonomy across seven DAG topologies with closed-form expressions for the $K$-class case, identifying which topologies preserve and which break the sign-flip guarantee.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifier errors in causal studies.}\n\\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error, noting matrix adjustment correction but without formal bias theorems. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \\citet{yang_adomavicius2018} formalized the two-stage pipeline problem (data mining followed by econometric analysis) and proved that ML-generated variables systematically bias downstream regression. \\citet{keith_jensen_oconnor2020} surveyed the text-and-causal-inference landscape, identifying classifier measurement error as a key open challenge. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers on clinical data but covered only one topology (unobserved confounding). \\citet{chen_bhattacharya_keith2024} used zero-shot NLP models as proxies in proximal causal inference, proving identification conditions for one DAG structure. We generalize across all seven topologies with multi-class confusion matrices, providing both bias expressions and sensitivity analysis tools absent from prior work.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM annotation quality.}\nRecent work has quantified the downstream risks of LLM annotation. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated across 37 tasks that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations. \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed that LLM errors are 60\\% correlated across models, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance via linear mixed models but explicitly excluded causal identification. \\citet{stolwijk_boukes_trilling2025} found that LLM annotations differ from human annotations in prevalence even when per-sample accuracy is adequate, and \\citet{cheng_mayya_sedoc2024} decomposed annotation measurement error into four source types. These works diagnose the annotation quality problem; our contribution provides the causal-analytic bridge connecting annotation error structure to downstream causal estimate validity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}\nOur ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures, and \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds. \\citet{vanderweele_li2019} extended sensitivity analysis to differential measurement error but only for binary settings. \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy (``how much bias would be needed to explain away the finding?'') to annotation-induced bias, with three complementary measures tailored to different topology-dependent failure modes.\n\n\\paragraph{Correction methods.}\nPrediction-powered inference~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are complementary to our framework: our topology-dependent ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.\n",

````json
{
  "text": "## 审查请求：Introduction + Related Work 叙事重构\n\n**审查范围**：Introduction（introduction.tex）和 Related Work（related_work.tex）的叙事改进。本轮只需审查这两个章节的叙事质量，不需审查实验数据或引用验证。\n\n**改动摘要**：\n\n### Introduction 改动（4 项）\n1. **\"Why now\" + multi-class 普遍性**：第 1 段新增 \"stance detection uses 3–4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more\"\n2. **F1≠causal validity 核心洞察提前**：第 2 段末尾新增 \"within a narrow F1 range of 0.614–0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies—from 4.1% to 42.1%—demonstrating that topology, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk\"\n3. **Contribution 3 重写**：先陈述 negative result（FWL invariance → 4/7 topologies 无法校正），再给 positive result（PPI++ 93.8%）\n4. **Kim & Garg 融入第 1 段**：从独立段落移入第 1 段 motivation，与 Baumann 31% 统计紧密衔接\n\n### Related Work 改动（3 项）\n1. **显式 gap statement**：每段末尾明确指出前人工作缺什么、我们的框架解决什么\n2. **ASV vs E-value 核心差异**：新增 \"rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification δ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold\"\n3. **Text classifier + LLM annotation quality 合并**：5 段 → 4 段，消除重叠\n\n### 当前 LaTeX 内容\n\n**introduction.tex:**\n```latex\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models---and these errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err)~\\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nThe consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\\% of confusion matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\\% (13.7\\% violation rate at diagonal $\\geq 0.7$). Moreover, standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies---from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%---demonstrating that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.\n\nWhy does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \\geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \\emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a surprising negative result: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance theorem proves that matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies---the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\\end{enumerate}\n```\n\n**related_work.tex:**\n```latex\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\\cite{gustafson2004}. \\citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \\citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \\citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. However, none of these works systematically characterize how bias varies \\emph{across} DAG topologies for a given confusion matrix. Our taxonomy fills this gap: given the same $K$-class confusion matrix, we derive how bias direction and magnitude differ across seven canonical topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}\n\\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models, and \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but explicitly excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework bridges this gap: we provide the causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}\nOur ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures: the minimum strength of unmeasured confounding needed to explain away an observed association. \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy to a different bias source: rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification $\\delta$ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold---with three complementary measures tailored to topology-dependent failure modes (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction methods.}\nPrediction-powered inference~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.\n```\n\n### 审查重点\n1. **叙事流畅度**：Introduction 的 4 段是否形成连贯论证链（problem → consequences → why → contributions）？\n2. **Motivation 强度**：F1≠causal validity 的预告是否足够有冲击力？Kim&Garg 融入是否自然？\n3. **Gap statement 有效性**：Related Work 每段末尾的 gap statement 是否精准、是否避免了泛泛的 \"our work extends\"？\n4. **ASV vs E-value 对比清晰度**：读者是否能立即理解 ASV 与 E-value 的本质区别？\n5. **Contribution 3 的 negative-first 结构**：FWL invariance 作为 surprising result 是否足够突出？\n6. **AAAI 审稿视角**：这两个章节能否帮助论文在 AAAI 获得 motivation 和 novelty 方面的高分？",
  "project": "causal_annot_error_attrib"
}
````

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models---and these errors are highly correlated across models (60\% agreement when both err)~\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.
5	
6	The consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\% of confusion matrices cause \emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\% (13.7\% violation rate at diagonal $\geq 0.7$). Moreover, standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies---from 4.1\% to 42.1\%---demonstrating that \emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.
7	
8	Why does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.
9	
10	We address these gaps with three contributions:
11	
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a surprising negative result: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance theorem proves that matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies---the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies.
18	\end{enumerate}
19	
```

    "newString": "relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%), illustrating that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err)~\\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nThe consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\\% of confusion matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\\% (13.7\\% violation rate at diagonal $\\geq 0.7$). Moreover, standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies---from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%---demonstrating that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.\n\nWhy does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \\geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \\emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a surprising negative result: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance theorem proves that matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies---the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a negative result with significant practical implications that, while theoretically predicted by Frisch--Waugh--Lovell, has been overlooked in the annotation correction literature: matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies, because the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err)~\\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nThe consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\\% of confusion matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\\% (13.7\\% violation rate at diagonal $\\geq 0.7$). Moreover, standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%), illustrating that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.\n\nWhy does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \\geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \\emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone. The taxonomy extends to binarization: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying up to 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a surprising negative result: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance theorem proves that matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies---the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels. We further show that collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014VR1RiKcaoi8qf33Qn7Ug9`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}
5	The epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\cite{gustafson2004}. \citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. However, none of these works systematically characterize how bias varies \emph{across} DAG topologies for a given confusion matrix. Our taxonomy fills this gap: given the same $K$-class confusion matrix, we derive how bias direction and magnitude differ across seven canonical topologies.
6	
7	\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}
8	\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\% error correlation across models, and \citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but explicitly excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework bridges this gap: we provide the causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.
9	
10	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}
11	Our ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures: the minimum strength of unmeasured confounding needed to explain away an observed association. \citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy to a different bias source: rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification $\delta$ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold---with three complementary measures tailored to topology-dependent failure modes (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip).
12	
13	\paragraph{Correction methods.}
14	Prediction-powered inference~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \emph{when} correction is needed and \emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \emph{how}.
15	
```

</details>

[Director 指令] [Patch] plan_006 全量跨K实证分析完成（D016），§4.6 + conclusion + abstract 需要更新。这次不用验证引用。

**背景数据（来自 registry checkpoint，已验证）**：
- 14 configs: humaid K=10 (8 configs, 3 frontier models × 3 prompts, 1 excluded) + civil_comments K=2 (3 configs, Qwen2.5-7B × 3 prompts) + vast K=3 (3 configs, Qwen2.5-7B × 3 prompts)
- 4 models: Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat (frontier, via API) + Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (local inference fallback)
- K=2 mean bias 36.9%, IV bias 116.5%
- K=3 mean bias 65.3%, IV bias 252.3%
- K=10 mean bias 12.2%, IV bias 30.3%
- F1 disconnect: civil_comments F1=0.72-0.75 but IV bias 101-131%
- 0 sign flips across all 98 bias scenarios (14×7)
- 7B vs 235B bias ratio: 1.24（模型规模差异可控）
- ASV-unsafe: 43.8% (92/210)
- humaid-only ASV-unsafe was 49.2% (31/63)

**修改 1: §4.6 (experiments.tex) — 扩展为跨K实证验证**

当前 §4.6 仅覆盖 HumAID (K=10)。需要：

a) 更新开头段落：从"We validate... using real LLM confusion matrices estimated from HumAID" 扩展为介绍 3 个数据集（HumAID K=10 + VAST K=3 + Civil Comments K=2）和 4 个 LLM。说明 humaid 用 3 个 frontier 模型，vast/civil_comments 用 Qwen2.5-7B 本地推理（因 API 余额不足）。

b) 保留当前 HumAID 段落内容（theory-practice alignment, ASV diagnostics, confusion matrix structure），但作为 "K=10" 子段落。

c) 新增一个 **Cross-K comparison** 段落，核心 narrative：
- K 决定 bias 严重程度：K=3 VAST 的 IV bias 高达 252.3%（vs K=10 HumAID 的 30.3%），因为更粗的分类粒度 → confusion matrix 偏离对角占优更严重
- K=2 Civil Comments：IV bias 116.5%，F1=0.72-0.75 但 IV bias 101-131% —— 确认 F1 disconnect 跨 K 成立
- 0 sign flips 跨所有 K（结构性保证不依赖 K）
- 7B vs 235B 比较：bias ratio 1.24，说明 topology 而非 model scale 是 bias 的主要决定因素

d) 注意 caveat：VAST/Civil Comments 使用 7B 本地模型而非 frontier models，与 HumAID 存在 model-tier asymmetry。这不影响主要结论（topology dominance），但需诚实声明。

e) 更新 fig5 caption 和数据：如果可能，提一句"full cross-K results in supplementary"或将 14 configs 全部画进 fig5。如果篇幅紧张，保持 fig5 为 HumAID-only 但在文中引用 cross-K 数据。

f) 更新 L112（ASV 数量）：全量是 92/210 (43.8%) ASV-unsafe，不再是 31/63 (49.2%)。

**修改 2: conclusion.tex**

a) "validation at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}2$ is ongoing" → 改为已完成，写实际结果（K determines bias severity + F1 disconnect confirmed across K）。

b) "developing a practitioner toolkit that computes ASVs..." → 改为"We release an open-source Python toolkit (annot-sensitivity) that computes ASVs from estimated confusion matrices and routes to topology-appropriate correction methods." plan_010 已完成。

**修改 3: abstract.tex（可选，如有空间）**

在 abstract 末尾 "Neither classification F1..." 之前加一句提及跨K实证验证：类似 "Empirical validation on three CSS tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$) with four LLMs confirms the theoretical predictions and reveals that $K$ determines bias severity."

**约束**：
- AAAI 8 页限制（main text）。如果 §4.6 扩展超限，优先压缩 cross-K 段落为 4-5 行，保留关键数字。
- 不要编造数据。上面给的数字来自 registry checkpoint，可信。如需更细粒度的 per-topology 数据，标 placeholder 让我确认。
- 编译确认 PASS 后报告。
