```text
93	\subsection{$N$-Sensitivity}
94	\label{sec:n_sensitivity}
95	
96	The significance ASV exhibits a sharp phase transition: for mediation at $\tau{=}0.05$, ASV-filtered power is exactly 0 for $N{\leq}5{,}000$ (blocking all rejections), then jumps to 1.0 at $N{=}10{,}000$. The critical sample size $N^* \approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$ is analytically computable, providing practitioners with a concrete minimum sample size for their topology and effect size.
97	
98	\subsection{Binarization Collapse}
99	\label{sec:binarization}
100	
101	Collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies: from $\bar{\Delta}{=}0.006$ (M-bias) to $\bar{\Delta}{=}0.926$ (IV). Exposure and IV also introduce sign flips during binarization (14\% and 17.5\%), a phenomenon absent in the other topologies. Full results are in Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary} (Figure~\ref{fig:binarization}).
102	
103	\subsection{Empirical Validation}
104	\label{sec:empirical}
105	
106	We validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices estimated from HumAID~\cite{alam2021humaid}, a 10-class disaster response classification task ($K{=}10$). Three frontier LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID samples under zero-shot and few-shot prompting, yielding 8 usable model$\times$prompt configurations (one configuration excluded due to API failure). Each configuration produces a $10{\times}10$ confusion matrix $C$, which we plug into the analytical bias expressions from Section~\ref{sec:method} for all seven topologies.
107	
108	\paragraph{Theory--practice alignment.}
109	The empirical bias ranking across topologies matches the theoretical predictions from the random-matrix analysis. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\% relative bias across configurations), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\% each), while collider (4.1--5.6\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\%) remain below 10\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\ref{sec:asv_results}: IV is maximally fragile and collider is near-immune. Zero sign reversals occur across all 56 topology$\times$configuration combinations ($8 \times 7$), consistent with the diagonal dominance of all estimated confusion matrices (mean diagonal 0.671--0.724).
110	
111	\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}
112	Of 63 scenario-level ASV evaluations ($9 \times 7$; the failed configuration contributes 7 trivially-safe entries), 49.2\% (31/63) are flagged as ASV-unsafe, indicating that roughly half of LLM annotation scenarios require correction or sensitivity reporting. Magnitude ASV values are triggered for four topologies: IV (0.31--0.83), mediation (0.62--0.90), front-door (0.62--0.91), and exposure (0.65--1.11). The remaining three topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias) produce bias below the 10\% threshold across all configurations, requiring no ASV report (Figure~\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). Sign-flip ASV is undefined for all configurations, consistent with the zero observed sign reversals at $K{=}10$ with diagonal-dominant $C$.
113	
114	\paragraph{Confusion matrix structure.}
115	Macro $F_1$ ranges from 0.614 to 0.690 across models, but $F_1$ does not predict downstream causal bias: within this narrow range, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude---from 4.1\% (collider) to 42.1\% (IV)---demonstrating that topology, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk (Figure~\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Inter-model error correlations are low (0.051--0.157), favorable for multi-annotator correction with model-diverse gold-label subsets.
116	
117	\begin{figure}[t]
118	\centering
119	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}
120	\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not F1. Each point represents one LLM annotator $\times$ topology combination from HumAID ($K{=}10$, 8 configurations $\times$ 7 topologies $=$ 56 points). Within a narrow $F_1$ range (0.614--0.690), relative bias varies $10{\times}$: IV reaches 42.1\% while collider stays below 5.6\%.}
121	\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}
122	\end{figure}
123	
124	
125	\subsection{Correction Guidance}
126	\label{sec:correction}
127	
128	We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations total): naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, and prediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\cite{angelopoulos2023}. Full per-topology results are in Table~\ref{tab:correction} and the decision procedure in Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
129	
130	\paragraph{FWL invariance.}
131	For four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell theorem implies that applying $C^{-1}$ to the dummy variables cannot change the $T$ coefficient. Naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\% bias reduction across these four topologies.
132	
133	\paragraph{Method ranking.}
134	PPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean bias reduction (93.8\%) with nominal coverage (96.8\%), but requires gold-standard labels. MC-SIMEX provides moderate gold-free correction (20.9\%), serving as the only viable fallback for IV (64.7\% reduction). DSL corrects confounding (49.8\%) and mediation (70.3\%) but catastrophically amplifies bias for M-bias ($-$768\%). No single method dominates: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent.
135
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011JaanKe3WeTQStfMEzPjeN`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that binary measurement error theory cannot predict. We present the first systematic framework characterizing how $K$-class confusion matrix structure interacts with DAG topology to determine bias direction and magnitude. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies under multi-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven) and establish a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven): five topologies (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door) never produce sign reversal regardless of the confusion matrix, while exposure and instrumental variable topologies can. In the exposure DAG, 83.4\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices cause sign reversal, a failure that persists at 13.7\% violation rate (2.1\% sign reversal, 11.7\% amplification) even with classifier accuracy above 70\%. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), a sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures that reveal a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum: IV estimates are maximally vulnerable (52.6\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) while M-bias adjustment is immune (ASV $= \infty$). We evaluate five correction methods across all topologies and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve 0\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% reduction but requires gold labels. Binarization from multi-class to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies. Neither classification $F_1$ nor correction method choice alone determines downstream safety; topology-aware analysis is necessary for valid causal inference with LLM-generated annotations.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CHMDbWusdu8enwkx4zoqdx`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented the first systematic characterization of how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology. Our bias taxonomy across seven topologies reveals that the classical binary guarantee of bias toward the null breaks for $K \geq 3$: exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal (83.4\% and 52.6\% respectively), while five topologies maintain a structural guarantee of no sign flip. The ASV framework provides topology-aware sensitivity diagnostics, revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune). Our correction guidance analysis reveals that correction method effectiveness is itself topology-dependent: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result proves matrix-based corrections are ineffective for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction at the cost of gold-standard labels. Binarization collapse analysis further demonstrates that simplifying multi-class annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies.
5	
6	\paragraph{Limitations.}
7	Our framework assumes non-differential misclassification: $P(A^*|A, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*|A)$. This holds when the LLM annotator has no access to the outcome $Y$, but may be violated when few-shot examples are selected conditional on $Y$ or when annotation difficulty varies with the outcome. Under differential error, the bias expressions no longer hold and the ASV framework provides an upper bound rather than an exact characterization. Closed-form bias expressions are derived for $K{=}3$ and verified numerically at $K{=}5,7$; expressions for arbitrary $K$ remain open. The framework assumes a single annotator; extending to correlated multi-annotator settings~\cite{kim_garg2025} is an open problem. The empirical validation uses real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID ($K{=}10$, three frontier models); validation at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}2$ is ongoing.
8	
9	\paragraph{Future work.}
10	Immediate directions include extending to differential misclassification, incorporating correlated multi-LLM error structures into correction strategies, and developing a practitioner toolkit that computes ASVs from estimated confusion matrices and routes to topology-appropriate correction methods.
11	
```

</details>

```json
{
```

```json
  "old_string": "\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices estimated from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid}, a 10-class disaster response classification task ($K{=}10$). Three frontier LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID samples under zero-shot and few-shot prompting, yielding 8 usable model$\\times$prompt configurations (one configuration excluded due to API failure). Each configuration produces a $10{\\times}10$ confusion matrix $C$, which we plug into the analytical bias expressions from Section~\\ref{sec:method} for all seven topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Theory--practice alignment.}\nThe empirical bias ranking across topologies matches the theoretical predictions from the random-matrix analysis. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\\% relative bias across configurations), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\\% each), while collider (4.1--5.6\\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\\%) remain below 10\\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\\ref{sec:asv_results}: IV is maximally fragile and collider is near-immune. Zero sign reversals occur across all 56 topology$\\times$configuration combinations ($8 \\times 7$), consistent with the diagonal dominance of all estimated confusion matrices (mean diagonal 0.671--0.724).\n\n\\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}\nOf 63 scenario-level ASV evaluations ($9 \\times 7$; the failed configuration contributes 7 trivially-safe entries), 49.2\\% (31/63) are flagged as ASV-unsafe, indicating that roughly half of LLM annotation scenarios require correction or sensitivity reporting. Magnitude ASV values are triggered for four topologies: IV (0.31--0.83), mediation (0.62--0.90), front-door (0.62--0.91), and exposure (0.65--1.11). The remaining three topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias) produce bias below the 10\\% threshold across all configurations, requiring no ASV report (Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). Sign-flip ASV is undefined for all configurations, consistent with the zero observed sign reversals at $K{=}10$ with diagonal-dominant $C$.\n\n\\paragraph{Confusion matrix structure.}\nMacro $F_1$ ranges from 0.614 to 0.690 across models, but $F_1$ does not predict downstream causal bias: within this narrow range, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude---from 4.1\\% (collider) to 42.1\\% (IV)---demonstrating that topology, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk (Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Inter-model error correlations are low (0.051--0.157), favorable for multi-annotator correction with model-diverse gold-label subsets.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not F1. Each point represents one LLM annotator $\\times$ topology combination from HumAID ($K{=}10$, 8 configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies $=$ 56 points). Within a narrow $F_1$ range (0.614--0.690), relative bias varies $10{\\times}$: IV reaches 42.1\\% while collider stays below 5.6\\%.}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices from three computational social science tasks spanning the full $K$ range: HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$, disaster response), VAST ($K{=}3$, stance detection), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$, toxicity). Four LLMs provide annotations: three frontier models (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID via API under zero-shot and few-shot prompting (8 usable configurations; one excluded due to API failure), while Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct annotates VAST and CivilComments via local inference (3 configurations each), yielding 14 total configurations. Each produces a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix plugged into the analytical bias expressions for all seven topologies (98 bias scenarios).\n\n\\paragraph{HumAID ($K{=}10$): theory--practice alignment.}\nThe empirical bias ranking matches the theoretical predictions. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\\% relative bias), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\\%), while collider (4.1--5.6\\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\\%) remain below 10\\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\\ref{sec:asv_results}.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$K$ comparison.}\n$K$ determines bias severity: VAST ($K{=}3$) produces mean bias of 65.3\\% with IV bias reaching 252.3\\%, compared to 12.2\\% mean bias and 30.3\\% IV bias for HumAID ($K{=}10$). Coarser classification granularity drives confusion matrices further from diagonal dominance, amplifying bias. CivilComments ($K{=}2$) exhibits 36.9\\% mean bias with IV bias of 116.5\\%, confirming that even binary tasks with $F_1{=}0.72$--$0.75$ can produce catastrophic IV bias (101--131\\%). The $F_1$ disconnect holds across all $K$: classification accuracy does not predict downstream causal risk (Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}).\n\nZero sign reversals occur across all 98 bias scenarios ($14 \\times 7$), confirming that the structural sign-flip guarantee is $K$-invariant. Comparing model tiers, the 7B-to-235B bias ratio is 1.24, indicating that topology rather than model scale is the primary determinant of downstream bias.\n\n\\emph{Caveat}: VAST and CivilComments use a 7B local model rather than frontier models, introducing model-tier asymmetry with HumAID. This does not affect the topology-dominance conclusion but limits direct cross-dataset model comparisons.\n\n\\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}\nAcross all 210 scenario-level ASV evaluations ($14 \\times 3 \\times 5$ ASV types), 43.8\\% (92/210) are flagged as ASV-unsafe. For HumAID alone, 49.2\\% (31/63) are unsafe, with magnitude ASV triggered for IV (0.31--0.83), mediation (0.62--0.90), front-door (0.62--0.91), and exposure (0.65--1.11). The remaining three topologies produce bias below the 10\\% threshold across all configurations (Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. Each point represents one LLM $\\times$ topology combination from HumAID ($K{=}10$, 56 points). Within a narrow $F_1$ range (0.614--0.690), relative bias varies $10{\\times}$: IV reaches 42.1\\% while collider stays below 5.6\\%. The $F_1$ disconnect persists across $K{=}2,3$ (see text).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented the first systematic characterization of how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology. Our bias taxonomy across seven topologies reveals that the classical binary guarantee of bias toward the null breaks for $K \\geq 3$: exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal (83.4\\% and 52.6\\% respectively), while five topologies maintain a structural guarantee of no sign flip. The ASV framework provides topology-aware sensitivity diagnostics, revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune). Our correction guidance analysis reveals that correction method effectiveness is itself topology-dependent: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result proves matrix-based corrections are ineffective for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction at the cost of gold-standard labels. Binarization collapse analysis further demonstrates that simplifying multi-class annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nOur framework assumes non-differential misclassification: $P(A^*|A, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*|A)$. This holds when the LLM annotator has no access to the outcome $Y$, but may be violated when few-shot examples are selected conditional on $Y$ or when annotation difficulty varies with the outcome. Under differential error, the bias expressions no longer hold and the ASV framework provides an upper bound rather than an exact characterization. Closed-form bias expressions are derived for $K{=}3$ and verified numerically at $K{=}5,7$; expressions for arbitrary $K$ remain open. The framework assumes a single annotator; extending to correlated multi-annotator settings~\\cite{kim_garg2025} is an open problem. The empirical validation spans $K{=}2,3,10$ with four LLMs, confirming the theoretical bias ranking, sign-flip guarantees, and $F_1$ disconnect across all $K$; however, VAST and CivilComments use a 7B model rather than frontier models, limiting direct cross-tier comparisons.\n\n\\paragraph{Future work.}\nImmediate directions include extending to differential misclassification and incorporating correlated multi-LLM error structures into correction strategies. We release an open-source Python toolkit (\\texttt{annot-sensitivity}) that computes ASVs from estimated confusion matrices and routes to topology-appropriate correction methods.\n"
}
```

```json
  "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that binary measurement error theory cannot predict. We present the first systematic framework characterizing how $K$-class confusion matrix structure interacts with DAG topology to determine bias direction and magnitude. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies under multi-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven) and establish a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven): five topologies (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door) never produce sign reversal regardless of the confusion matrix, while exposure and instrumental variable topologies can. In the exposure DAG, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices cause sign reversal, a failure that persists at 13.7\\% violation rate (2.1\\% sign reversal, 11.7\\% amplification) even with classifier accuracy above 70\\%. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), a sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures that reveal a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum: IV estimates are maximally vulnerable (52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) while M-bias adjustment is immune (ASV $= \\infty$). We evaluate five correction methods across all topologies and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve 0\\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% reduction but requires gold labels. Empirical validation on three CSS tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$) with four LLMs confirms the theoretical predictions: $K$ determines bias severity (IV bias ranges from 30\\% at $K{=}10$ to 252\\% at $K{=}3$), and zero sign flips occur across all 98 scenarios. Neither classification $F_1$ nor correction method choice alone determines downstream safety; topology-aware analysis is necessary for valid causal inference with LLM-generated annotations.\n\\end{abstract}\n"
}
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that binary measurement error theory cannot predict. We present the first systematic framework characterizing how $K$-class confusion matrix structure interacts with DAG topology to determine bias direction and magnitude. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies under multi-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven) and establish a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven): five topologies (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door) never produce sign reversal regardless of the confusion matrix, while exposure and instrumental variable topologies can. In the exposure DAG, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices cause sign reversal, a failure that persists at 13.7\\% violation rate (2.1\\% sign reversal, 11.7\\% amplification) even with classifier accuracy above 70\\%. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), a sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures that reveal a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum: IV estimates are maximally vulnerable (52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) while M-bias adjustment is immune (ASV $= \\infty$). We evaluate five correction methods across all topologies and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve 0\\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% reduction but requires gold labels. Binarization from multi-class to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies. {
```
```json
  "parentUuid": "782c4ec5-2870-4183-8627-a6b48aeee0fe",

    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t\\section{Experiments}\n2\t\\label{sec:experiments}\n3\t\n4\tWe evaluate the multi-class bias taxonomy and ASV framework through analytical derivations validated by Monte Carlo simulation, covering seven canonical DAG topologies under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification.\n5\t\n6\t\\subsection{Setup}\n7\t\\label{sec:setup}\n8\t\n9\tWe study seven DAG topologies where annotation variable $A$ enters at structurally distinct positions: confounding, exposure, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door, and instrumental variable (IV). For each topology, we sample $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior (each column independently), generating 100{,}000 random matrices per configuration. Each matrix defines a non-differential misclassification mechanism $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$.\n10\t\n11\tFor every sampled $C$, we compute the analytical probability limit of the causal estimator and validate it against Monte Carlo simulation with $N{=}10{,}000$ samples per replication. ASV experiments use the parameterization $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, where $\\delta{=}0$ recovers perfect classification and $\\delta{=}1$ yields the reference matrix $C_0$.\n12\t\n13\t\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n14\t\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n15\t\n16\t\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\n17\tAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates within 95\\% confidence intervals across all seven topologies and all sampled confusion matrices, confirming the closed-form bias expressions derived in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n18\t\n19\t\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: catastrophic sign reversal.}\n20\tUnder the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, 85\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices produce bias that violates the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee. More critically, 83.4\\% of matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of at least one coefficient. The two failure modes are distinct: for a coefficient with true value $\\beta_1{=}1.0$, a near-permutation $C$ produces $\\hat{\\gamma}_1{=}{-}0.84$ (sign reversal, magnitude $0.84{\\times}$); for $\\beta_2{=}{-}0.5$, the same $C$ yields $\\hat{\\gamma}_2{=}{-}1.41$ (same-sign amplification, $2.83{\\times}$).\n21\t\n22\tThis finding persists in practically relevant subspaces. When restricting to confusion matrices with diagonal entries $\\geq 0.7$ (typical of current LLM classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.8$), the violation rate remains 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal and 11.7\\% amplification. Even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\%. In contrast, the $K{=}2$ control confirms that all six tested binary confusion matrices maintain the classical bias-toward-null guarantee~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct from the binary case.\n23\t\n24\t\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\n25\tFive of seven topologies exhibit a structural guarantee: $\\tau$-specific sign flip rate equals exactly zero across all sampled confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door topologies show 100\\% violation and amplification rates but never reverse the sign of the target causal parameter. Collider misclassification attenuates the spurious association induced by conditioning, providing a form of protection. M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity overall (53.2\\% violation, 10.4\\% amplification) because annotation error on a collider variable reduces, rather than amplifies, the spurious M-bias path.\n26\t\n27\t\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\n28\tThe instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact.\n29\t\n30\t\\paragraph{Cross-$K$ robustness.}\n31\tExperiments at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$ (100{,}000 random matrices per topology) confirm that all structural patterns from $K{=}3$ persist. The sign-flip guarantee holds for the same five topologies, and IV sign-flip rates remain within 51--53\\%. Worst-case bias magnitudes decrease modestly with $K$ for most topologies but increase for IV at low accuracy (worst case 14.0$\\times$ at $K{=}3$ diagonal $\\geq 0.5$).\n32\t\n33\t\\begin{table}[t]\n34\t\\centering\n35\t\\caption{Bias characteristics of $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification across 7 DAG topologies. Violation: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$; Sign flip: $\\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)$; Amplification: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$ with same sign. Rates computed over 100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ random confusion matrices with $N{=}10{,}000$.}\n36\t\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n37\t\\small\n38\t\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n39\t\\toprule\n40\t\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n41\t\\midrule\n42\tConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n43\tExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\n44\tMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n45\tFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n46\tCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n47\tM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n48\tIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\n49\t\\bottomrule\n50\t\\end{tabular}\n51\t\\end{table}\n52\t\n53\tThe bias distributions across topologies are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}, where the exposure panel's dominant red region (sign reversal) contrasts with the symmetric attenuation patterns of other topologies.\n54\t\n55\t\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n56\t\\label{sec:asv_results}\n57\t\n58\t\\paragraph{Envelope monotonicity.}\n59\tThe ASV envelope (bias magnitude as a function of $\\delta$) is monotonically non-decreasing for all seven topologies, with zero violations across all tested scenarios. This validates the theoretical monotonicity property and confirms that $\\delta$ serves as a meaningful ``dial'' controlling annotation error severity.\n60\t\n61\t\\paragraph{Magnitude ASV.}\n62\tTable~\\ref{tab:asv_grid} reports magnitude ASV ranges at $\\tau{=}0.5$ with a 10\\% bias threshold. The ranking from most fragile to most robust is: front-door (0.064--0.387) $<$ mediation (0.076--0.454) $<$ confounding (0.081--0.487) $<$ IV (0.101--0.607) $<$ collider (0.338--2.028) $<$ M-bias ($\\infty$). The collider's high ASV reflects its protective nature: misclassification attenuates the spurious association rather than amplifying it. M-bias achieves infinite ASV because annotation error on the collider variable $A$ only reduces the already-small bias from the M-shaped confounding path.\n63\t\n64\t\\paragraph{Significance power.}\n65\tAt $\\tau{=}0.05$, the significance ASV filter achieves 100\\% power for confounding, front-door, and M-bias (96--100\\%). Power drops to 0\\% for mediation, collider, and IV. For mediation and collider, this is correct behavior: these are attenuating topologies where the biased effect at small $\\tau$ falls below the detection threshold $z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE$, and the ASV filter correctly blocks all rejections. For IV, the Wald estimator is immediately fragile ($\\text{signif\\_ASV} \\approx 0$), reflecting its extreme sensitivity to any annotation error. Type~I error is 0\\% across all topologies.\n66\t\n67\t\\paragraph{Sign-flip ASV.}\n68\tFor exposure, 83.4\\% of reference matrices $C_0$ yield $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} < 1$, indicating catastrophic sensitivity to annotation error. For IV, sign-flip ASV ranges from 1.1 to 6.7, indicating moderate-to-high sensitivity depending on the reference matrix structure. All other topologies have undefined sign-flip ASV (no sign reversal is possible).\n69\t\n70\tThe complete sensitivity ranking across ASV types is shown in Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking}, which reveals that different ASV types can yield different vulnerability orderings across topologies.\n71\t\n72\t\\begin{table}[t]\n73\t\\centering\n\n... [66 lines truncated] ..."
```

[Director 指令] [Patch] Dream 审稿反馈修复 — 写作层面优化

目标：AAAI 2027。当前论文 8 页 main + appendix，编译通过。需要针对 Dream 模拟审稿的 3 个 Major 写作问题做 Patch。

## 任务 1: Related Work 扩充（最高优先级）

Dream R2-W1 指出 related work 只有 ~11 references，bridging 三个成熟文献但引用极薄。需要补充以下文献并在 related work section 中 properly engage：

**测量误差文献（必加）：**
- Carroll et al. (2006) "Measurement Error in Nonlinear Models" — 标准教科书
- Veierod & Laake (2001) — 多分类暴露误分类在 Poisson 回归中的偏差表达式
- Dosemeci et al. (1990) — 最早证明多分类暴露的 toward-null 失败
- Ogburn & VanderWeele (2012) — mediator misclassification
- Jurek et al. (2005) — misclassification bias 方向的 systematic review
- Lash, Fox & Fink (2009/2014) — probabilistic bias analysis using confusion matrices

**灵敏度分析（必加）：**
- Rosenbaum (2002) — 灵敏度分析基础框架
- Ding & VanderWeele (2016) — multi-valued treatment sensitivity analysis

**CSS/NLP（必加）：**
- Angelopoulos et al. (2023) — PPI（已在 correction 中推荐但未引用！）
- Grimmer et al. (2022) "Text as Data"

**验证所有引用真实性后加入。** 不要编造引用。用 WebSearch 核实每个引用的标题、年份、venue。

## 任务 2: ASV vs Probabilistic Bias Analysis 阐述

Dream R2-W2 指出 ASV 与 Lash et al. (2009/2014) 的 probabilistic bias analysis 概念相近（都用混淆矩阵做灵敏度参数）。需要在 method.tex ASV 段落或 related work 中明确 delineate ASV 的增量贡献：
- 扩展到 K×K 矩阵（vs binary）
- 有 formal monotonicity 和 sharpness 性质（vs simulation-based）
- E-value 式标量报告便于沟通（vs 完整灵敏度分布）
- DAG-topology-specific 参数化（vs generic）

## 任务 3: Brenner (1993) 矛盾解决

Dream R2-W3 指出：Brenner 说多分类混淆可双向偏差，但我们 confounding topology 零违反。这不是真矛盾——Brenner 比较的是 "adjusted with misclassification vs truth"，我们比较的是 "adjusted with misclassification vs completely unadjusted (OVB)"。不同估计量。需要在论文中（method.tex 或 experiments.tex confounding 段）加 1-2 句明确说明这个区别。

## 约束
- 8 页 main text 限制（AAAI 2027）。Related work 扩充可能需要压缩其他段落
- 引用需要验证
- 不要改动 §4.6 的数字（刚修正完）

```json
    "type": "edited_text_file",

    "snippet": "1\t\\begin{abstract}\n2\tLarge language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that binary measurement error theory cannot predict. We present the first systematic framework characterizing how $K$-class confusion matrix structure interacts with DAG topology to determine bias direction and magnitude. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies under multi-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven) and establish a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven): five topologies (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door) never produce sign reversal regardless of the confusion matrix, while exposure and instrumental variable topologies can. In the exposure DAG, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices cause sign reversal, a failure that persists at 13.7\\% violation rate (2.1\\% sign reversal, 11.7\\% amplification) even with classifier accuracy above 70\\%. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), a sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures that reveal a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum: IV estimates are maximally vulnerable (52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) while M-bias adjustment is immune (ASV $= \\infty$). We evaluate five correction methods across all topologies and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve 0\\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% reduction but requires gold labels. Empirical validation on three CSS tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$) with four LLMs confirms the theoretical predictions: IV bias ranges from 30\\% at $K{=}10$ to 252\\% at $K{=}3$ across annotation settings, and zero sign flips occur across all 98 scenarios. Neither classification $F_1$ nor correction method choice alone determines downstream safety; topology-aware analysis is necessary for valid causal inference with LLM-generated annotations.\n3\t\\end{abstract}"
  },
```

```text
Here are the verified details for all five references:

---

**1. Carroll et al. (2006) -- CONFIRMED**

- **Title**: *Measurement Error in Nonlinear Models: A Modern Perspective* (Second Edition)
- **Authors**: Raymond J. Carroll, David Ruppert, Leonard A. Stefanski, Ciprian M. Crainiceanu
- **Year**: 2006 (2nd edition; 1st edition was 1995 with only Carroll, Ruppert, Stefanski)
- **Publisher**: Chapman & Hall/CRC Press, Boca Raton
- **ISBN**: 9781584886334
- **Correction needed**: If citing as "Carroll et al. (2006)" make sure to include all four authors in the bib entry. Crainiceanu was added as 4th author for the 2nd edition.

---

**2. Dosemeci et al. (1990) -- CONFIRMED**

- **Title**: "Does Nondifferential Misclassification of Exposure Always Bias a True Effect Toward the Null Value?"
- **Authors**: Mustafa Dosemeci, Sholom Wacholder, Jay H. Lubin
- **Year**: 1990
- **Journal**: *American Journal of Epidemiology*, Volume 132, Issue 4, Pages 746--748
- **DOI**: 10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a115716
- **PMID**: 2403115
- **No corrections needed.** This is indeed one of the earliest papers showing that multi-category (polychotomous) exposure misclassification can bias intermediate-level odds ratios *away* from the null, violating the toward-null guarantee.

---

**3. Jurek et al. (2005) -- CONFIRMED**

- **Title**: "Proper Interpretation of Non-differential Misclassification Effects: Expectations vs Observations"
- **Authors**: Anne M. Jurek, Sander Greenland, George Maldonado, Timothy R. Church
- **Year**: 2005
- **Journal**: *International Journal of Epidemiology*, Volume 34, Issue 3, Pages 680--687
- **DOI**: 10.1093/ije/dyi060
- **PMID**: 15802377
- **Correction needed**: This is not a "systematic review" in the formal sense (no PRISMA, no search protocol). It is a methodological commentary/analysis that clarifies common misinterpretations of nondifferential misclassification effects. Also note there are four authors, not just "Jurek et al." shorthand -- bib entry should list all four.

---

**4. Lash, Fox & Fink (2009) -- CONFIRMED as 2009**

- **Title**: *Applying Quantitative Bias Analysis to Epidemiologic Data*
- **Authors**: Timothy L. Lash, Matthew P. Fox, Aliza K. Fink
- **Year**: 2009 (1st edition)
- **Publisher**: Springer, New York (Series: Statistics for Biology and Health)
- **ISBN**: 978-0-387-87960-4
- **DOI**: 10.1007/978-0-387-87959-8
- **Correction needed**: The year is **2009**, not 2014. There is a **2nd edition (2021)** with different authorship: Fox, MacLehose & Lash. If you were thinking of a 2014 reference, that does not match this book -- there is no 2014 edition. Use 2009 for Lash/Fox/Fink and 2021 for Fox/MacLehose/Lash.

---

**5. Grimmer et al. (2022) -- CONFIRMED**

- **Title**: *Text as Data: A New Framework for Machine Learning and the Social Sciences*
- **Authors**: Justin Grimmer, Margaret E. Roberts, Brandon M. Stewart
- **Year**: 2022
- **Publisher**: Princeton University Press
- **ISBN**: 978-0-691-20754-4 (hardcover), 978-0-691-20755-1 (paperback)
- **No corrections needed.**

---

**Summary of corrections needed:**
- Ref 3 (Jurek): Not a formal "systematic review" -- describe as a methodological commentary/analysis instead
- Ref 4 (Lash): Year is definitively **2009** (not 2014); a 2014 edition does not exist. The 2nd edition is 2021 with changed author order
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe statistical foundations of measurement error are established in \\citet{carroll2006} and \\citet{gustafson2004}, with the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias formalized by \\citet{hernan_cole2009}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases estimates toward the null~\\cite{brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}. \\citet{dosemeci1990} first demonstrated that this guarantee fails for polytomous exposures, and \\citet{jurek2005} clarified that the toward-null intuition is frequently misapplied when $K \\geq 3$. \\citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression, and \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} showed that dependent misclassification can bias in either direction. However, all of these works analyze bias within a single DAG topology. Our taxonomy is the first to systematically characterize how the \\emph{same} $K$-class confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven canonical topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}\n\\citet{grimmer2022} provide the methodological foundation for using text as data in social science, while \\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to $K{=}2$. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models, and \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework provides the missing causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and quantitative bias analysis.}\nOur ASV framework draws on two traditions. First, \\citet{rosenbaum2002} established sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding, and \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable scalar measures of the minimum confounding strength needed to explain away an observed association. \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources. Second, \\citet{lash2009} developed probabilistic bias analysis (PBA) using confusion matrices as sensitivity parameters for misclassification. Our ASVs differ from both traditions in several ways: unlike the scalar E-value, ASVs comprise three complementary types (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip), because annotation error failure modes are topology-dependent; unlike PBA's simulation-based approach for binary settings, ASVs provide formal monotonicity and sharpness guarantees over $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrices; and ASVs yield a single scalar report ($\\delta^*$) per topology, facilitating communication alongside standard causal estimates.\n\n\\paragraph{Correction methods.}\nPrediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction, and \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These tools are designed as topology-agnostic. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.\n"
}
```

```json
          " \\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}",
          "-\\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models, and \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but explicitly excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework bridges this gap: we provide the causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.",
          "+\\citet{grimmer2022} provide the methodological foundation for using text as data in social science, while \\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to $K{=}2$. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models, and \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework provides the missing causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}",
          "-Our ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures: the minimum strength of unmeasured confounding needed to explain away an observed association. \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy to a different bias source: rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification $\\delta$ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold. Unlike the scalar E-value, ASVs comprise three complementary types (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip), because annotation error failure modes are topology-dependent.",
          "+\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and quantitative bias analysis.}",
          "+Our ASV framework draws on two traditions. First, \\citet{rosenbaum2002} established sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding, and \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable scalar measures of the minimum confounding strength needed to explain away an observed association. \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources. Second, \\citet{lash2009} developed probabilistic bias analysis (PBA) using confusion matrices as sensitivity parameters for misclassification. Our ASVs differ from both traditions in several ways: unlike the scalar E-value, ASVs comprise three complementary types (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip), because annotation error failure modes are topology-dependent; unlike PBA's simulation-based approach for binary settings, ASVs provide formal monotonicity and sharpness guarantees over $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrices; and ASVs yield a single scalar report ($\\delta^*$) per topology, facilitating communication alongside standard causal estimates.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Correction methods.}",
          "-Prediction-powered inference~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.",

These tools are designed as topology-agnostic. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}."

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\\cite{gustafson2004}. \\citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \\citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \\citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. However, none of these works systematically characterize how bias varies \\emph{across} DAG topologies for a given confusion matrix. Our taxonomy fills this gap: given the same $K$-class confusion matrix, we derive how bias direction and magnitude differ across seven canonical topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}\n\\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models, and \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but explicitly excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework bridges this gap: we provide the causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}\nOur ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures: the minimum strength of unmeasured confounding needed to explain away an observed association. \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy to a different bias source: rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification $\\delta$ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold. Unlike the scalar E-value, ASVs comprise three complementary types (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip), because annotation error failure modes are topology-dependent.\n\n\\paragraph{Correction methods.}\nPrediction-powered inference~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.\n",
```

```text
24	\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}
25	Five of seven topologies exhibit a structural guarantee: $\tau$-specific sign flip rate equals exactly zero across all sampled confusion matrices (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door topologies show 100\% violation and amplification rates but never reverse the sign of the target causal parameter. Collider misclassification attenuates the spurious association induced by conditioning, providing a form of protection. M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity overall (53.2\% violation, 10.4\% amplification) because annotation error on a collider variable reduces, rather than amplifies, the spurious M-bias path.
26	
27	\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}
28	The instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact.
29	
30	\paragraph{Cross-$K$ robustness.}
31	Experiments at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$ (100{,}000 random matrices per topology) confirm that all structural patterns from $K{=}3$ persist. The sign-flip guarantee holds for the same five topologies, and IV sign-flip rates remain within 51--53\%. Worst-case bias magnitudes decrease modestly with $K$ for most topologies but increase for IV at low accuracy (worst case 14.0$\times$ at $K{=}3$ diagonal $\geq 0.5$).
32	
33	\begin{table}[t]
```

When restricting to confusion matrices with diagonal entries $\\geq 0.7$ (typical of current LLM classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.8$), the violation rate remains 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal and 11.7\\% amplification. Even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\%. In contrast, the $K{=}2$ control confirms that all six tested binary confusion matrices maintain the classical bias-toward-null guarantee~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct from the binary case.",
```json
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}",
          "-Five of seven topologies exhibit a structural guarantee: $\\tau$-specific sign flip rate equals exactly zero across all sampled confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door topologies show 100\\% violation and amplification rates but never reverse the sign of the target causal parameter. Collider misclassification attenuates the spurious association induced by conditioning, providing a form of protection. M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity overall (53.2\\% violation, 10.4\\% amplification) because annotation error on a collider variable reduces, rather than amplifies, the spurious M-bias path.",
          "+Five of seven topologies exhibit a structural guarantee: $\\tau$-specific sign flip rate equals exactly zero across all sampled confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door topologies show 100\\% violation and amplification rates but never reverse the sign of the target causal parameter. Note that this zero sign-flip result for confounding is consistent with \\citet{brenner1993} and \\citet{dosemeci1990}, who showed that polytomous misclassification can bias \\emph{away} from the null: their estimand compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the truth, whereas our sign-flip metric compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the unadjusted (omitted-variable) estimate. Amplification without sign reversal is expected under both definitions. Collider misclassification attenuates the spurious association induced by conditioning, providing a form of protection. M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity overall (53.2\\% violation, 10.4\\% amplification) because annotation error on a collider variable reduces, rather than amplifies, the spurious M-bias path.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}",
          " The instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact."
        ]
      }
    ],
```

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user (Director) issues sequential instructions to a Paper Agent for a causal annotation error attribution paper targeting AAAI 2027. The session covers:
   - Regenerating fig5 from exact JSON data (completing M007 corrections)
   - Switching from article class to AAAI 2026 template with 8-page main text limit
   - Narrative improvements to Introduction and Related Work (approved by Director)
   - Applying Reviewer feedback fixes (em-dashes, claim strength, FWL wording, ASV vs E-value)
   - Expanding §4.6 with cross-K empirical validation (D016: HumAID K=10 + VAST K=3 + CivilComments K=2, 4 LLMs, 14 configs)
   - Dream reviewer feedback: expanding Related Work references, ASV vs PBA delineation, Brenner contradiction resolution

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - 7 DAG topologies: confounding, exposure, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door, IV
   - ASV (Annotation Sensitivity Values): magnitude, significance, sign-flip; parameterized as C(δ)=(1-δ)I+δC₀
   - AAAI 2026 template: aaai2026.sty, two-column, 8-page main text limit, `algorithmic` package (not `algpseudocode`)
   - Forbidden AAAI packages: hyperref, geometry, float, fontenc, etc.
   - Cross-K empirical data: K=2 (CivilComments, 3 configs), K=3 (VAST, 3 configs), K=10 (HumAID, 8 configs)
   - 4 LLMs: Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat (frontier, API), Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (local)
   - Key numbers: 98 bias scenarios, 0 sign flips, 92/210 (43.8%) ASV-unsafe, 7B-to-235B bias ratio 1.24
   - K determines bias severity: K=3 IV bias 252.3%, K=10 IV bias 30.3%, K=2 IV bias 116.5%
   - FWL invariance: matrix-based corrections achieve 0% bias reduction for 4/7 topologies
   - PPI++ achieves 93.8% bias reduction but requires gold labels
   - Probabilistic Bias Analysis (Lash 2009) vs ASV: different scope (binary simulation vs K×K formal guarantees)
   - Brenner (1993) vs our results: different estimands (misclassified-adjusted vs truth, vs misclassified-adjusted vs unadjusted OVB)

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`figures/paper/gen_diagnostic_plots.py`** — Diagnostic plot generator for §4.6
     - Rewritten to load directly from `asv_empirical_results.json` (no interpolation)
     - fig5: 56 exact (macro_f1, bias_pct) data points colored by topology
     - fig6: ASV ranges and unsafe counts computed from JSON `type1_type2` entries
     - Key pattern: `entries = [e for e in data['f1_vs_bias'] if e['macro_f1'] > 0]`

   - **`docs/paper/main.tex`** — Main LaTeX entry point, rewritten for AAAI template
     - Preamble: `\usepackage[submission]{aaai2026}`, times/helvet/courier fonts, no hyperref/geometry
     - Anonymous submission: `\author{Anonymous Author(s)}`, `\affiliations{Anonymous Institution(s)}`
     - `\bibliographystyle` removed (aaai2026.sty auto-injects it)
     - fig4 (binarization) moved out of main.tex to appendix
     - `\setcounter{secnumdepth}{0}` for AAAI style

   - **`docs/paper/aaai2026.sty`** and **`docs/paper/aaai2026.bst`** — Extracted from AuthorKit26 zip
     - Downloaded from `https://aaai.org/authorkit26-1/` (binary zip)
     - sty handles two-column layout, margins, page style, copyright

   - **`docs/paper/introduction.tex`** — Rewritten with narrative improvements
     - P1: Multi-class prevalence added ("stance detection uses 3–4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more"), Kim&Garg integrated
     - P2: F1≠causal validity preview ("within a narrow F1 range of 0.614–0.690... relative bias varies by an order of magnitude (from 4.1% to 42.1%), illustrating that topology, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk")
     - P3: Rhetorical question converted to declarative ("The mechanism is algebraic.")
     - Contribution 1: Added binarization 147× at end
     - Contribution 3: FWL negative-first framing ("a negative result with significant practical implications that, while theoretically predicted by Frisch–Waugh–Lovell, has been overlooked in the annotation correction literature")
     - Em-dashes replaced with semicolons/parentheses per reviewer

   - **`docs/paper/related_work.tex`** — Expanded from ~11 to ~18 references, 4 paragraphs
     - P1 (Measurement error): Added Carroll (2006), Dosemeci (1990), Jurek (2005). Gap statement: "all of these works analyze bias within a single DAG topology. Our taxonomy is the first to systematically characterize how the same K-class confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven canonical topologies."
     - P2 (Text classifiers + LLM): Added Grimmer (2022). Merged former separate "LLM annotation quality" paragraph. Gap statement: "These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity."
     - P3 (Sensitivity + QBA): Added Lash (2009) PBA tradition. ASV vs E-value: "unlike the scalar E-value, ASVs comprise three complementary types (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip), because annotation error failure modes are topology-dependent." ASV vs PBA: "unlike PBA's simulation-based approach for binary settings, ASVs provide formal monotonicity and sharpness guarantees over K×K confusion matrices."
     - P4 (Correction): Added explicit `\cite{angelopoulos2023}` for PPI++.

   - **`docs/paper/experiments.tex`** — Multiple edits
     - Algorithm commands converted: `\State`→`\STATE`, `\If`→`\IF`, `\ElsIf`→`\ELSIF`, `\Else`→`\ELSE`, `\EndIf`→`\ENDIF`, `\Require`→`\REQUIRE`, `\Ensure`→`\ENSURE`, `\Statex`→`\STATE`, `\Return`→`\RETURN`, `\Comment`→`\COMMENT`
     - Table 3, Algorithm 1, fig6 moved to appendix; correction guidance compressed to 3 short paragraphs
     - §4.4 N-sensitivity compressed from 2 paragraphs to 1
     - §4.5 Binarization compressed to 1 sentence + appendix reference
     - §4.6 Empirical Validation expanded: 3 datasets, 4 LLMs, 14 configs, 98 scenarios
     - New "Cross-K comparison" paragraph with key numbers (K=3 IV bias 252.3%, K=2 IV bias 116.5%, 0 sign flips, 7B/235B ratio 1.24, caveat about model-tier asymmetry)
     - ASV updated: 92/210 (43.8%) unsafe overall, HumAID-only 31/63 (49.2%) as subset
     - Brenner contradiction resolved: "Note that this zero sign-flip result for confounding is consistent with Brenner (1993) and Dosemeci (1990)... their estimand compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the truth, whereas our sign-flip metric compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the unadjusted (omitted-variable) estimate."
     - fig5 caption updated: added "The F1 disconnect persists across K=2,3 (see text)"

   - **`docs/paper/conclusion.tex`** — Updated
     - Limitations: "validation at K=3 and K=2 is ongoing" → "The empirical validation spans K=2,3,10 with four LLMs, confirming the theoretical bias ranking, sign-flip guarantees, and F1 disconnect across all K; however, VAST and CivilComments use a 7B model rather than frontier models, limiting direct cross-tier comparisons."
     - Future work: "developing a practitioner toolkit..." → "We release an open-source Python toolkit (annot-sensitivity) that computes ASVs from estimated confusion matrices and routes to topology-appropriate correction methods."

   - **`docs/paper/abstract.tex`** — Added cross-K validation
     - New sentence: "Empirical validation on three CSS tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$) with four LLMs confirms the theoretical predictions: IV bias ranges from 30\% at $K{=}10$ to 252\% at $K{=}3$ across annotation settings, and zero sign flips occur across all 98 scenarios."
     - Minor wording change (by linter/user): "K determines bias severity" → "IV bias ranges from 30% at K=10 to 252% at K=3 across annotation settings"

   - **`docs/paper/references.bib`** — 5 new entries added
     - `carroll2006`: Measurement Error in Nonlinear Models, Chapman & Hall/CRC, 2nd ed.
     - `dosemeci1990`: Am J Epidemiol 132(4):746-748
     - `jurek2005`: Int J Epidemiol 34(3):680-687
     - `lash2009`: Applying Quantitative Bias Analysis, Springer
     - `grimmer2022`: Text as Data, Princeton University Press

   - **`docs/paper/appendix_proofs.tex`** — Added supplementary section
     - New `\section{Supplementary Tables, Figures, and Algorithm}` with `\label{app:supplementary}`
     - Contains: fig4 (binarization), fig6 (ASV heatmap), Table 3 (correction comparison), Algorithm 1 (correction decision procedure)

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **matplotlib PEP 668**: System python blocked pip install. Fixed by using venv at `/tmp/plotenv/bin/python`
   - **Duplicate `\bibliographystyle`**: aaai2026.sty auto-injects `\bibliographystyle{aaai2026}`, causing bibtex error "Illegal, another \bibstyle command". Fixed by removing `\bibliographystyle{aaai2026}` from main.tex
