      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate the multi-class bias taxonomy and ASV framework through analytical derivations validated by Monte Carlo simulation, covering seven canonical DAG topologies under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification.\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study seven DAG topologies where annotation variable $A$ enters at structurally distinct positions: confounding, exposure, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door, and instrumental variable (IV). For each topology, we sample $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior (each column independently), generating 100{,}000 random matrices per configuration. Each matrix defines a non-differential misclassification mechanism $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$.\n\nFor every sampled $C$, we compute the analytical probability limit of the causal estimator and validate it against Monte Carlo simulation with $N{=}10{,}000$ samples per replication. ASV experiments use the parameterization $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, where $\\delta{=}0$ recovers perfect classification and $\\delta{=}1$ yields the reference matrix $C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates within 95\\% confidence intervals across all seven topologies and all sampled confusion matrices, confirming the closed-form bias expressions derived in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: catastrophic sign reversal.}\nUnder the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, 85\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices produce bias that violates the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee. More critically, 83.4\\% of matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of at least one coefficient. The two failure modes are distinct: for a coefficient with true value $\\beta_1{=}1.0$, a near-permutation $C$ produces $\\hat{\\gamma}_1{=}{-}0.84$ (sign reversal, magnitude $0.84{\\times}$); for $\\beta_2{=}{-}0.5$, the same $C$ yields $\\hat{\\gamma}_2{=}{-}1.41$ (same-sign amplification, $2.83{\\times}$).\n\nThis finding persists in practically relevant subspaces. When restricting to confusion matrices with diagonal entries $\\geq 0.7$ (typical of current LLM classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.8$), the violation rate remains 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal and 11.7\\% amplification. Even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\%. In contrast, the $K{=}2$ control confirms that all six tested binary confusion matrices maintain the classical bias-toward-null guarantee~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct from the binary case.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive of seven topologies exhibit a structural guarantee: $\\tau$-specific sign flip rate equals exactly zero across all sampled confusion matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door topologies show 100\\% violation and amplification rates but never reverse the sign of the target causal parameter. Note that this zero sign-flip result for confounding is consistent with \\citet{brenner1993} and \\citet{dosemeci1990}, who showed that polytomous misclassification can bias \\emph{away} from the null: their estimand compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the truth, whereas our sign-flip metric compares the misclassified-adjusted estimate to the unadjusted (omitted-variable) estimate. Amplification without sign reversal is expected under both definitions. Collider misclassification attenuates the spurious association induced by conditioning, providing a form of protection. M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity overall (53.2\\% violation, 10.4\\% amplification) because annotation error on a collider variable reduces, rather than amplifies, the spurious M-bias path.\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$K$ robustness.}\nExperiments at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$ (100{,}000 random matrices per topology) confirm that all structural patterns from $K{=}3$ persist. The sign-flip guarantee holds for the same five topologies, and IV sign-flip rates remain within 51--53\\%. Worst-case bias magnitudes decrease modestly with $K$ for most topologies but increase for IV at low accuracy (worst case 14.0$\\times$ at $K{=}3$ diagonal $\\geq 0.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias characteristics of $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification across 7 DAG topologies. Violation: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$; Sign flip: $\\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)$; Amplification: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$ with same sign. Rates computed over 100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ random confusion matrices with $N{=}10{,}000$.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions across topologies are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}, where the exposure panel's dominant red region (sign reversal) contrasts with the symmetric attenuation patterns of other topologies.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Envelope monotonicity.}\nThe ASV envelope (bias magnitude as a function of $\\delta$) is monotonically non-decreasing for all seven topologies, with zero violations across all tested scenarios. This validates the theoretical monotonicity property and confirms that $\\delta$ serves as a meaningful ``dial'' controlling annotation error severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Magnitude ASV.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:asv_grid} reports magnitude ASV ranges at $\\tau{=}0.5$ with a 10\\% bias threshold. The ranking from most fragile to most robust is: front-door (0.064--0.387) $<$ mediation (0.076--0.454) $<$ confounding (0.081--0.487) $<$ IV (0.101--0.607) $<$ collider (0.338--2.028) $<$ M-bias ($\\infty$). The collider's high ASV reflects its protective nature: misclassification attenuates the spurious association rather than amplifying it. M-bias achieves infinite ASV because annotation error on the collider variable $A$ only reduces the already-small bias from the M-shaped confounding path.\n\n\\paragraph{Significance power.}\nAt $\\tau{=}0.05$, the significance ASV filter achieves 100\\% power for confounding, front-door, and M-bias (96--100\\%). Power drops to 0\\% for mediation, collider, and IV. For mediation and collider, this is correct behavior: these are attenuating topologies where the biased effect at small $\\tau$ falls below the detection threshold $z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE$, and the ASV filter correctly blocks all rejections. For IV, the Wald estimator is immediately fragile ($\\text{signif\\_ASV} \\approx 0$), reflecting its extreme sensitivity to any annotation error. Type~I error is 0\\% across all topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Sign-flip ASV.}\nFor exposure, 83.4\\% of reference matrices $C_0$ yield $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} < 1$, indicating catastrophic sensitivity to annotation error. For IV, sign-flip ASV ranges from 1.1 to 6.7, indicating moderate-to-high sensitivity depending on the reference matrix structure. All other topologies have undefined sign-flip ASV (no sign reversal is possible).\n\nThe complete sensitivity ranking across ASV types is shown in Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking}, which reveals that different ASV types can yield different vulnerability orderings across topologies.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{ASV type applicability and sensitivity across DAG topologies. Magnitude ASV at $\\tau{=}0.5$, 10\\% threshold. Power at $\\tau{=}0.05$ significance filter. \\checkmark: effective and recommended; ($\\checkmark$): applicable but less informative; ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_grid}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag. ASV} & \\textbf{Signif.} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} & \\textbf{Rank} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV & 0.101--0.607 & 0\\% & 1.1--6.7 & 1 (fragile) \\\\\nFront-door & 0.064--0.387 & 100\\% & --- & 2 \\\\\nConfounding & 0.081--0.487 & 100\\% & --- & 3 \\\\\nMediation & 0.076--0.454 & 0\\% & --- & 4 \\\\\nCollider & 0.338--2.028 & 0\\% & --- & 5 \\\\\nM-bias & $\\infty$ & 96--100\\% & --- & 6 (immune) \\\\\nExposure & --- & --- & 83.4\\%$^*$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\scriptsize $^*$Fraction of $C_0$ with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} < 1$. Exposure uses sign-flip ASV exclusively.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{$N$-Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:n_sensitivity}\n\nThe significance ASV exhibits a sharp phase transition: for mediation at $\\tau{=}0.05$, ASV-filtered power is exactly 0 for $N{\\leq}5{,}000$ (blocking all rejections), then jumps to 1.0 at $N{=}10{,}000$. The critical sample size $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$ is analytically computable, providing practitioners with a concrete minimum sample size for their topology and effect size.\n\n\\subsection{Binarization Collapse}\n\\label{sec:binarization}\n\nCollapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\\times$ across topologies: from $\\bar{\\Delta}{=}0.006$ (M-bias) to $\\bar{\\Delta}{=}0.926$ (IV). Exposure and IV also introduce sign flips during binarization (14\\% and 17.5\\%), a phenomenon absent in the other topologies. Full results are in Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary} (Figure~\\ref{fig:binarization}).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices from three computational social science tasks spanning the full $K$ range: HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$, disaster response), VAST ($K{=}3$, stance detection), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$, toxicity). Four LLMs provide annotations: three frontier models (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID via API under zero-shot and few-shot prompting (8 usable configurations; one excluded due to API failure), while Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct annotates VAST and CivilComments via local inference (3 configurations each), yielding 14 total configurations. Each produces a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix plugged into the analytical bias expressions for all seven topologies (98 bias scenarios).\n\n\\paragraph{HumAID ($K{=}10$): theory--practice alignment.}\nThe empirical bias ranking matches the theoretical predictions. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\\% relative bias), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\\%), while collider (4.1--5.6\\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\\%) remain below 10\\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\\ref{sec:asv_results}.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$K$ comparison.}\nBias varies substantially across annotation settings: VAST ($K{=}3$) produces mean bias of 65.3\\% with IV bias reaching 252.3\\%, compared to 12.2\\% mean bias and 30.3\\% IV bias for HumAID ($K{=}10$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$) exhibits 36.9\\% mean bias with IV bias of 116.5\\%. These differences reflect the combined effects of classification granularity ($K$), confusion matrix quality (diagonal dominance), and dataset difficulty; the three factors co-vary across datasets and cannot be attributed to $K$ alone. Nonetheless, even binary tasks with $F_1{=}0.72$--$0.75$ can produce catastrophic IV bias (101--131\\%), confirming that classification accuracy does not predict downstream causal risk (Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}).\n\nZero sign reversals occur across all 98 bias scenarios ($14 \\times 7$), confirming that the structural sign-flip guarantee is $K$-invariant.\\footnote{Four additional valid frontier-model configurations for VAST (Llama-4-Maverick $\\times 3$, DeepSeek-Chat $\\times 1$) also exhibit zero sign flips, extending this result to 126 scenarios across 18 configurations.} Comparing model tiers, the 7B-to-235B bias ratio is 1.24, indicating that topology rather than model scale is the primary determinant of downstream bias.\n\n\\emph{Caveat}: VAST and CivilComments use a 7B local model rather than frontier models, introducing model-tier asymmetry with HumAID. Additionally, VAST's confusion matrix exhibits near-degenerate structure (third-class recall $= 4.6\\%$), which may disproportionately drive the extreme IV bias observed at $K{=}3$. These factors limit direct cross-dataset comparisons but do not affect the topology-dominance conclusion within each dataset.\n\n\\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}\nFor HumAID ($K{=}10$, 8 valid configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies $=$ 56 scenarios), 55.4\\% (31/56) are flagged as magnitude-ASV-unsafe, with magnitude ASV triggered for IV (8/8 configs, ASV $=$ 0.31--0.83), mediation (8/8, ASV $=$ 0.62--0.90), front-door (8/8, ASV $=$ 0.62--0.91), and exposure (7/8, ASV $=$ 0.65--1.11). Confounding, collider, and M-bias remain below the 10\\% bias threshold across all configurations (Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). For CivilComments ($K{=}2$), 100\\% of scenarios (21/21) are ASV-unsafe despite $F_1{=}0.72$--$0.75$, underscoring that classification accuracy alone cannot guarantee downstream safety.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. Each point represents one LLM $\\times$ topology combination from HumAID ($K{=}10$, 56 points). Within a narrow $F_1$ range (0.614--0.690), relative bias varies $10{\\times}$: IV reaches 42.1\\% while collider stays below 5.6\\%. The $F_1$ disconnect persists across $K{=}2,3$ (see text).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations total): naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, and prediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}. Full per-topology results are in Table~\\ref{tab:correction} and the decision procedure in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nFor four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell theorem implies that applying $C^{-1}$ to the dummy variables cannot change the $T$ coefficient. Naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\\% bias reduction across these four topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean bias reduction (93.8\\%) with nominal coverage (96.8\\%), but requires gold-standard labels. MC-SIMEX provides moderate gold-free correction (20.9\\%), serving as the only viable fallback for IV (64.7\\% reduction). DSL corrects confounding (49.8\\%) and mediation (70.3\\%) but catastrophically amplifies bias for M-bias ($-$768\\%). However, MC-SIMEX is harmful for front-door ($-$55.7\\%) and exposure ($-$3.8\\%), and should only be applied after topology identification. No single method dominates: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent.\n",

        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe statistical foundations of measurement error are established in \\citet{carroll2006} and \\citet{gustafson2004}, with the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias formalized by \\citet{hernan_cole2009}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases estimates toward the null~\\cite{brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}. \\citet{dosemeci1990} first demonstrated that this guarantee fails for polytomous exposures, and \\citet{jurek2005} clarified that the toward-null intuition is frequently misapplied when $K \\geq 3$. \\citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression, and \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} showed that dependent misclassification can bias in either direction. However, all of these works analyze bias within a single DAG topology. Our taxonomy is the first to systematically characterize how the \\emph{same} $K$-class confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven canonical topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}\n\\citet{grimmer2022} provide the methodological foundation for using text as data in social science, while \\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \\citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to $K{=}2$. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation across models, and \\citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework provides the missing causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and quantitative bias analysis.}\nOur ASV framework draws on two traditions. First, \\citet{rosenbaum2002} established sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding, and \\citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable scalar measures of the minimum confounding strength needed to explain away an observed association. \\citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \\citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources. Second, \\citet{lash2009} developed probabilistic bias analysis (PBA) using confusion matrices as sensitivity parameters for misclassification. Our ASVs differ from both traditions in several ways: unlike the scalar E-value, ASVs comprise three complementary types (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip), because annotation error failure modes are topology-dependent; unlike PBA's simulation-based approach for binary settings, ASVs provide formal monotonicity and sharpness guarantees over $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrices; and ASVs yield a single scalar report ($\\delta^*$) per topology, facilitating communication alongside standard causal estimates.\n\n\\paragraph{Correction methods.}\nPrediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels. \\citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction, and \\citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These tools are designed as topology-agnostic. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \\emph{when} correction is needed and \\emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \\emph{how}.\n",

{

    "type": "file",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that binary measurement error theory cannot predict. We present the first systematic framework characterizing how $K$-class confusion matrix structure interacts with DAG topology to determine bias direction and magnitude. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies under multi-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven) and establish a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven): five topologies (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door) never produce sign reversal regardless of the confusion matrix, while exposure and instrumental variable topologies can. In the exposure DAG, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices cause sign reversal, a failure that persists at 13.7\\% violation rate (2.1\\% sign reversal, 11.7\\% amplification) even with classifier accuracy above 70\\%. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), a sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures that reveal a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum: IV estimates are maximally vulnerable (52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) while M-bias adjustment is immune (ASV $= \\infty$). We evaluate five correction methods across all topologies and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve 0\\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% reduction but requires gold labels. Empirical validation on three CSS tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$) with four LLMs confirms the theoretical predictions: IV bias ranges from 30\\% at $K{=}10$ to 252\\% at $K{=}3$ across annotation settings, and zero sign flips occur across all 98 scenarios. Neither classification $F_1$ nor correction method choice alone determines downstream safety; topology-aware analysis is necessary for valid causal inference with LLM-generated annotations.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Critically, the primary treatment coefficient is never reversed.\"\n- 重点：**primary never reverses** 必须在 Introduction 中出现，不能只在 Section 4.3\n- 第 2 段和第 3 段可能需要合并或重新排序\n\n**注意**：Abstract 已在之前修改中删除了 83.4%，不需要动。只改 Introduction。\n\n## 修改 2：ASV 贡献重新定位 [W6 MAJOR]\n\n**文件**：`introduction.tex` + `method.tex`\n\n**问题**：ASV 被放在 contribution #2 的 C 位，但 R4 审稿人质疑它是 E-value 的直接移植。\n\n**修改方向**：\n\n**introduction.tex** 的 contribution 列表重排：\n1. **Multi-class bias taxonomy**（保持 #1，不变）\n2. **Topology-aware correction guidance + FWL 不变性**（从 #3 提升到 #2——这是 genuine novel negative result，审稿人一致认可）\n3. **Annotation Sensitivity Values**（从 #2 降到 #3——重新定位为理论分析的自然推论）\n\n**method.tex** Section 3.3 ASV 引入段（第 74-79 行附近）：\n- 当前：\"We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs) as a unified sensitivity analysis framework, analogous to E-values...\"\n- 改为：\"Our bias taxonomy and FWL invariance results naturally motivate a sensitivity analysis tool: how much annotation error can a study tolerate before conclusions change? _source line 3725 · 2026-05-22T15:25:39.398Z_

[服务器重启后首条消息] 当前日期: 2026-05-22。服务器刚重启，项目状态可能已更新。不要依赖对话历史中的状态信息，以 registry 当前值为准。

**用户消息：** [Director 指令] [Patch] Round 4 审稿修复 — 83.4% framing 重写 + ASV 贡献重新定位 + Introduction 贡献层级重排

**投稿目标**：EMNLP 2026 via ARR，Long Paper，8 页 main text
**核心 claim**：K≥3 代数断裂 + 拓扑依赖性系统刻画（theory-first），FWL 不变性（surprise negative result），ASV 作为自然推论
**实验完成度**：100%（23 configs × 7 topologies = 161 scenarios）
**引用不需要验证**

---

## 修改 1：Introduction 83.4% framing 重写 [W2 CRITICAL]

**文件**：`introduction.tex`

**问题**：第 2 段把 83.4% 作为动机句（"Across the full Dirichlet(1,1,1) parameter space, sign reversal reaches 83.4%"），但 (1) primary coefficient never reverses，(2) realistic regime 只有 2.1%，(3) 83.4% 来自含近置换矩阵的极端参数空间。连续 4 轮审稿都指出这是 CRITICAL overclaim。

**修改方向**：
- 第 2 段开头改为先说 qualitative break："The transition at K≥3 is not gradual but structural: for K=2, attenuation toward the null is an algebraic identity..."（现有第 3 段的内容提前）
- 83.4% 不作为动机，而是作为 K=2→K≥3 断裂的**量化证据**："This structural break is quantifiable: under uniform Dirichlet prior spanning the full parameter space, 83.4% of K=3 exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of secondary coefficients (0% at K=2); under realistic accuracy (diagonal ≥ 0.7), 2.1% do so. Critically, the primary treatment coefficient is never reversed."
- 重点：**primary never reverses** 必须在 Introduction 中出现，不能只在 Section 4.3
- 第 2 段和第 3 段可能需要合并或重新排序

**注意**：Abstract 已在之前修改中删除了 83.4%，不需要动。只改 Introduction。

## 修改 2：ASV 贡献重新定位 [W6 MAJOR]

**文件**：`introduction.tex` + `method.tex`

**问题**：ASV 被放在 contribution #2 的 C 位，但 R4 审稿人质疑它是 E-value 的直接移植。

**修改方向**：

**introduction.tex** 的 contribution 列表重排：
1. **Multi-class bias taxonomy**（保持 #1，不变）
2. **Topology-aware correction guidance + FWL 不变性**（从 #3 提升到 #2——这是 genuine novel negative result，审稿人一致认可）
3. **Annotation Sensitivity Values**（从 #2 降到 #3——重新定位为理论分析的自然推论）

**method.tex** Section 3.3 ASV 引入段（第 74-79 行附近）：
- 当前："We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs) as a unified sensitivity analysis framework, analogous to E-values..."
- 改为："Our bias taxonomy and FWL invariance results naturally motivate a sensitivity analysis tool: how much annotation error can a study tolerate before conclusions change? We define Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs) for this purpose, drawing on the E-value framework~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} but with key technical differences..."
- 然后明确列举 3 个技术区别：(1) E-value parametrize scalar Γ 天然单调，ASV 将 K×K matrix 映射到 1D path 需证明 ε-approximate monotonicity；(2) 三种 ASV 类型 vs E-value 单类型；(3) ASV + FWL → correction routing（E-value 无此 channel）

---

修改后确保 8 页限制不被突破（当前刚好 8 页）。编译确认无 warning。

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\% agreement when both err)~\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.
5	
6	Even under realistic classifier accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), 13.7\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices violate the classical binary guarantee, with 2.1\% causing sign reversal of the estimated treatment effect. Across the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ parameter space, sign reversal reaches 83.4\%. This breaks the guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. Standard classification metrics do not predict this risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\% to 42.1\%), illustrating that \emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.
7	
8	The mechanism is algebraic, and the transition at $K {\geq} 3$ is a qualitative break, not a gradual degradation. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ to preserve coefficient signs---an algebraic property of the $2{\times}2$ matrix dimension, not of a specific error rate. This guarantee \emph{vanishes} at $K {\geq} 3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal even when every diagonal entry exceeds $0.5$~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). The possibility of polytomous sign reversal was noted by \citet{dosemeci1990} and \citet{veirod_laake2001}; our contribution extends this to a systematic characterization across seven DAG topologies and provides the ASV framework for quantifying vulnerability.
9	
10	We address these gaps with three contributions:
11	
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification, prove sign preservation for confounding and collider, verify it numerically for mediation, M-bias, and front-door, and show that exposure and IV can produce sign reversal. Binarization amplifies the gap: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying 154$\times$ across topologies.
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose three complementary sensitivity measures---magnitude, significance, and sign-flip ASV---revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\% sign reversal) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$).
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods and show that matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. PPI++ achieves 47.0\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\%).
18	\end{enumerate}
19	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup and Notation}
5	\label{sec:notation}
6	
7	Let $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one. Following \citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.
8	
9	\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]
10	\label{def:nondiff}
11	Misclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.
12	\end{definition}
13	
14	This assumption is satisfied when the LLM does not observe outcome-related text during annotation---for instance, when treatment labels are assigned from source documents that exclude outcome measurements.
15	
16	We study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\cite{pearl2009} in which $A$ occupies structurally distinct causal roles: confounder, exposure, mediator, collider, M-bias variable, front-door mediator, and instrument. Each role induces a different functional relationship between $C$ and the probability limit of the standard causal estimator, yielding topology-dependent bias.
17	
18	\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}
19	\label{sec:bias_expressions}
20	
21	For each topology, we derive the probability limit of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\tau$ denote the target causal parameter and $\hat{\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.
22	
23	\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}
24	When $A$ confounds the $X{\to}Y$ relationship, the OLS estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
25	\begin{equation}
26	\label{eq:confounding}
27	\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(A^*) = \bigl(\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}Z]\bigr)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}X]\,\boldsymbol{\beta},
28	\end{equation}
29	where $Z = [X, \text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\hat{\tau}$ is preserved: $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\footnote{This result is consistent with \citet{brenner1993}, who showed that the adjusted estimate with misclassified polytomous confounders can be biased toward or away from the null. Both findings hold simultaneously: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing residual bias whose magnitude can exceed the correctly adjusted estimate (amplification), but the treatment effect sign is never reversed. The distinction is between \emph{sign reversal} ($\text{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \text{sign}(\tau)$, which we show never occurs) and \emph{amplification} ($|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$, which occurs universally in this topology).}
30	
31	\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}
32	When $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:
33	\begin{equation}
34	\label{eq:exposure}
35	\text{plim}\;\hat{\beta}_j(A^*) = \sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\,\beta_k,
36	\end{equation}
37	where $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the marginal distribution of $A$. Unlike the binary case, where non-differential misclassification guarantees attenuation toward the null~\cite{brenner1993}, the $K{\geq}3$ case permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \emph{sign reversal}. A near-permutation confusion matrix effectively swaps category labels, making sign reversal the dominant outcome (83.4\% under uniform Dirichlet prior). This failure mode is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$ because a $2{\times}2$ stochastic matrix with positive diagonal cannot reverse the sign of a single coefficient.
38	
39	\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}
40	When $A$ mediates the $X{\to}Y$ relationship, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
41	\begin{equation}
42	\label{eq:mediation}
43	\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}(A^*) = \tau_{\text{direct}} + \bigl(1 - \lambda(C)\bigr)\,\tau_{\text{indirect}},
44	\end{equation}
45	where $\lambda(C) \in [0,1]$ under the sign preservation condition $\operatorname{sign}(\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}) = \operatorname{sign}(\tau_{\mathrm{total}})$ (Appendix~\ref{app:proof}), with $\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$).
46	
47	\paragraph{Collider ($A$ = collider, conditioned on $A^*$).}
48	Conditioning on a collider $A$ of $X$ and $Y$ opens a spurious path. When we condition on $A^*$ instead, misclassification attenuates this spurious association because $A^*$ is a noisy proxy for $A$. The collider topology thus exhibits a \emph{protective} property: annotation error reduces rather than amplifies the bias from collider stratification, with $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$.
49	
50	\paragraph{M-bias ($A$ = intermediate collider).}
51	In the M-shaped DAG ($U_1 \to X$, $U_1 \to A$, $U_2 \to A$, $U_2 \to Y$, $X \to Y$), adjusting for $A$ introduces a small spurious association via the M-bias path~\cite{greenland2003}. Misclassifying $A$ weakens this adjustment, reducing the M-bias. This topology is \emph{immune}: $\hat{\tau}(A^*)$ is closer to $\tau$ than $\hat{\tau}(A)$ for all non-trivial confusion matrices, yielding $\text{ASV} = \infty$.
52	
53	\paragraph{Front-door ($A$ = front-door mediator).}
54	In the front-door DAG ($X \to A \to Y$ with unmeasured $U \to X$, $U \to Y$), the front-door estimator~\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} uses $A$ to identify effects despite unmeasured confounding. Misclassification attenuates both $X \to A$ and $A \to Y$ relationships, producing moderate bias with $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$.
55	
56	\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}
57	The Wald estimator~\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\hat{\tau}_{\text{IV}} = \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign, causing the ratio estimator to diverge or flip. Under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\% produce sign reversal, making IV the most fragile topology.
58	
59	\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}
60	\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation---analytic results]
61	\label{prop:signflip}
62	Under non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:
63	\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\operatorname{sign}(\tau) = \operatorname{sign}(\tau {+} B_{\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\mathrm{OV}} {=} \operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\ref{app:confounding}).
64	\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\tau| > \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g)\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T{\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\ref{app:collider}).
65	\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\ref{app:exposure}).
66	\end{proposition}
67	
68	Our theoretical results establish a three-tier hierarchy: (i)~analytically proved (Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}(a,b), Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}), (ii)~numerically verified with zero violations across 100{,}000 matrices (mediation, M-bias, front-door sign preservation), and (iii)~conjectured with $\varepsilon$-approximate bounds (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}).
69	
70	\paragraph{Numerical Verification 1} (Mediation, M-bias, front-door).
71	Sign preservation holds under topology-specific conditions: mediation requires $\operatorname{sign}(\tau_{\mathrm{dir}}) {=} \operatorname{sign}(\tau_{\mathrm{total}})$; front-door requires $\operatorname{sign}(\lambda\alpha_U) {=} \operatorname{sign}(\lambda\alpha_U {+} \operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)))$; M-bias is attenuating for all $C$. Verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations (Appendix~\ref{app:conditions}); analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open.
72	
73	These conditions involve true DGP parameters, which in practice must be estimated. The ASV framework (§\ref{sec:asv}) provides a complementary assessment that does not require knowledge of the exact sufficient conditions.
74	
75	\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}
76	\label{sec:asv}
77	
78	We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs) as a unified sensitivity analysis framework, analogous to E-values for unmeasured confounding~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} but designed for annotation-induced bias.
79	
80	\paragraph{Parameterization.}
81	We parameterize the confusion matrix as $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix (e.g., estimated from a validation set) and $\delta \geq 0$ controls the severity of misclassification. At $\delta{=}0$, classification is perfect; at $\delta{=}1$, classification matches $C_0$. Non-negativity of $C(\delta)$ requires $\delta \leq \delta_{\max} = \min_j \frac{1}{1 - (C_0)_{jj}}$. Since $(C_0)_{jj} \in [0,1]$, we have $\delta_{\max} \geq 1$ for all valid $C_0$, ensuring the full range $[0,1]$ is always feasible; all ASV computations are constrained to $[0, \delta_{\max}]$. In practice, $C_0$ is estimated from a finite validation set and subject to sampling uncertainty; we recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals. The linear path $C(\delta)$ is the natural first-order approximation; alternative parameterizations (e.g., matrix exponential or Dirichlet-based paths) could capture nonlinear degradation but sacrifice the interpretability of $\delta$ as a scalar severity dial.
82	
83	\paragraph{Three ASV types.}
84	We define three complementary measures: (1)~\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{mag}} = \min\{\delta : |\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta)) - \tau| > \epsilon\}$---analogous to E-values; (2)~\textbf{Significance ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sig}} = \min\{\delta : |\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta))| < z_{\alpha/2} \cdot SE(N)\}$---relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} = \min\{\delta : \text{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \text{sign}(\tau)\}$---exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.
85	
86	\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity---confounding and mediation]
87	\label{thm:monotone}
88	For confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$ for some scalar $\lambda$) under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\delta \in [0, \delta_{\max}]$.
89	\end{theorem}
90	
91	\emph{Proof sketch.} Both topologies reduce to $B(C(\delta)) {=} \lambda V_W(\delta)/(V_W(\delta) {+} \sigma^2)$ where $V_W(\delta) {=} \operatorname{Var}(g) {-} \operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g{\mid}A^*_\delta])$ and $\sigma^2$ is a topology-specific constant ($\sigma_T^2$ for confounding, $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T{\mid}A)]$ for mediation). As $\delta$ increases, $A^*$ becomes a noisier channel for $A$, so $\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb{E}[g{\mid}A^*_\delta])$ decreases by the data processing inequality, increasing $V_W$. Since $x/(x{+}c)$ is increasing for $c{>}0$, $|B|$ is non-decreasing (full proofs in Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof} and~\ref{par:med_monotone}). For general (non-proportional) effects, numerical verification across 10{,}000 random matrices confirms $\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity with $\varepsilon < 0.02$ (Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof}).
92	
93	\begin{conjecture}[$\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]
94	\label{conj:monotone}
95	For the four remaining non-IV topologies (exposure, collider, M-bias, front-door) under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$: exposure and front-door are $\varepsilon$-approximately non-decreasing in $\delta$, and collider and M-bias (protective topologies) are $\varepsilon$-approximately non-increasing in $\delta$, with $\varepsilon < 0.06$ ($<$6\% of $\tau$). Mediation envelope monotonicity is proved for proportional effects (Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}) and verified numerically for the general case with $\varepsilon < 0.02$. Verified across 10{,}000 random matrices per topology and $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ using analytic probability limits (Appendix~\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}).
96	\end{conjecture}
97	
98	The instrumental variable topology is excluded from Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}: the Wald estimator $\hat{\tau} = \hat{\gamma}_{Y{\sim}A^*} / \hat{\gamma}_{T{\sim}A^*}$ exhibits singularities when the first-stage coefficient passes through zero as $\delta$ increases, producing unbounded deviations ($>10^6$). This is a property of the Wald estimator itself, not of the ASV framework; magnitude ASV remains well-defined for IV away from first-stage singularities.
99	
100	When strict monotonicity holds only approximately ($\varepsilon < 0.06$), the reported ASV threshold $\delta^*$ may differ from the true threshold by at most $\varepsilon/\eta$ in $\delta$-units, where $\eta$ is the practitioner's bias tolerance. At the recommended $\eta = 10\%$, this uncertainty is below 0.6 $\delta$-steps---negligible for practical sensitivity assessment. Topology rankings (which topologies are most fragile) are preserved across all tested configurations.
101	
102	Binarization of $K{=}3$ annotations introduces additional topology-dependent bias (Appendix~\ref{app:binarization}).
103

      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Setup and Notation}\n\\label{sec:notation}\n\nLet $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one. Following \\citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \\emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]\n\\label{def:nondiff}\nMisclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis assumption is satisfied when the LLM does not observe outcome-related text during annotation---for instance, when treatment labels are assigned from source documents that exclude outcome measurements.\n\nWe study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\\cite{pearl2009} in which $A$ occupies structurally distinct causal roles: confounder, exposure, mediator, collider, M-bias variable, front-door mediator, and instrument. Each role induces a different functional relationship between $C$ and the probability limit of the standard causal estimator, yielding topology-dependent bias.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}\n\\label{sec:bias_expressions}\n\nFor each topology, we derive the probability limit of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\\tau$ denote the target causal parameter and $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}\nWhen $A$ confounds the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship, the OLS estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:confounding}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(A^*) = \\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}Z]\\bigr)^{-1}\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}X]\\,\\boldsymbol{\\beta},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $Z = [X, \\text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\\hat{\\tau}$ is preserved: $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\\footnote{This result is consistent with \\citet{brenner1993}, who showed that the adjusted estimate with misclassified polytomous confounders can be biased toward or away from the null. Both findings hold simultaneously: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing residual bias whose magnitude can exceed the correctly adjusted estimate (amplification), but the treatment effect sign is never reversed. The distinction is between \\emph{sign reversal} ($\\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)$, which we show never occurs) and \\emph{amplification} ($|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$, which occurs universally in this topology).}\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}\nWhen $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:exposure}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\beta}_j(A^*) = \\sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\\,\\beta_k,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the marginal distribution of $A$. Unlike the binary case, where non-differential misclassification guarantees attenuation toward the null~\\cite{brenner1993}, the $K{\\geq}3$ case permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \\emph{sign reversal}. A near-permutation confusion matrix effectively swaps category labels, making sign reversal the dominant outcome (83.4\\% under uniform Dirichlet prior). This failure mode is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$ because a $2{\\times}2$ stochastic matrix with positive diagonal cannot reverse the sign of a single coefficient.\n\n\\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}\nWhen $A$ mediates the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mediation}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}(A^*) = \\tau_{\\text{direct}} + \\bigl(1 - \\lambda(C)\\bigr)\\,\\tau_{\\text{indirect}},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$ under the sign preservation condition $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}) = \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}), with $\\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Collider ($A$ = collider, conditioned on $A^*$).}\nConditioning on a collider $A$ of $X$ and $Y$ opens a spurious path. When we condition on $A^*$ instead, misclassification attenuates this spurious association because $A^*$ is a noisy proxy for $A$. The collider topology thus exhibits a \\emph{protective} property: annotation error reduces rather than amplifies the bias from collider stratification, with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{M-bias ($A$ = intermediate collider).}\nIn the M-shaped DAG ($U_1 \\to X$, $U_1 \\to A$, $U_2 \\to A$, $U_2 \\to Y$, $X \\to Y$), adjusting for $A$ introduces a small spurious association via the M-bias path~\\cite{greenland2003}. Misclassifying $A$ weakens this adjustment, reducing the M-bias. This topology is \\emph{immune}: $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ is closer to $\\tau$ than $\\hat{\\tau}(A)$ for all non-trivial confusion matrices, yielding $\\text{ASV} = \\infty$.\n\n\\paragraph{Front-door ($A$ = front-door mediator).}\nIn the front-door DAG ($X \\to A \\to Y$ with unmeasured $U \\to X$, $U \\to Y$), the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} uses $A$ to identify effects despite unmeasured confounding. Misclassification attenuates both $X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$ relationships, producing moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}\nThe Wald estimator~\\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{IV}} = \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign, causing the ratio estimator to diverge or flip. Under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\\% produce sign reversal, making IV the most fragile topology.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}\n\\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation---analytic results]\n\\label{prop:signflip}\nUnder non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:\n\\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau) = \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau {+} B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\\mathrm{OV}} {=} \\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\\ref{app:confounding}).\n\\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\\tau| > \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T{\\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\\ref{app:collider}).\n\\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\\end{proposition}\n\nOur theoretical results establish a three-tier hierarchy: (i)~analytically proved (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}(a,b), Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}), (ii)~numerically verified with zero violations across 100{,}000 matrices (mediation, M-bias, front-door sign preservation), and (iii)~conjectured with $\\varepsilon$-approximate bounds (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}).\n\n\\paragraph{Numerical Verification 1} (Mediation, M-bias, front-door).\nSign preservation holds under topology-specific conditions: mediation requires $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}) {=} \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$; front-door requires $\\operatorname{sign}(\\lambda\\alpha_U) {=} \\operatorname{sign}(\\lambda\\alpha_U {+} \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)))$; M-bias is attenuating for all $C$. Verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations (Appendix~\\ref{app:conditions}); analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open.\n\nThese conditions involve true DGP parameters, which in practice must be estimated. The ASV framework (§\\ref{sec:asv}) provides a complementary assessment that does not require knowledge of the exact sufficient conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}\n\\label{sec:asv}\n\nWe introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs) as a unified sensitivity analysis framework, analogous to E-values for unmeasured confounding~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} but designed for annotation-induced bias.\n\n\\paragraph{Parameterization.}\nWe parameterize the confusion matrix as $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix (e.g., estimated from a validation set) and $\\delta \\geq 0$ controls the severity of misclassification. At $\\delta{=}0$, classification is perfect; at $\\delta{=}1$, classification matches $C_0$. Non-negativity of $C(\\delta)$ requires $\\delta \\leq \\delta_{\\max} = \\min_j \\frac{1}{1 - (C_0)_{jj}}$. Since $(C_0)_{jj} \\in [0,1]$, we have $\\delta_{\\max} \\geq 1$ for all valid $C_0$, ensuring the full range $[0,1]$ is always feasible; all ASV computations are constrained to $[0, \\delta_{\\max}]$. In practice, $C_0$ is estimated from a finite validation set and subject to sampling uncertainty; we recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals. The linear path $C(\\delta)$ is the natural first-order approximation; alternative parameterizations (e.g., matrix exponential or Dirichlet-based paths) could capture nonlinear degradation but sacrifice the interpretability of $\\delta$ as a scalar severity dial.\n\n\\paragraph{Three ASV types.}\nWe define three complementary measures: (1)~\\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta)) - \\tau| > \\epsilon\\}$---analogous to E-values; (2)~\\textbf{Significance ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sig}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta))| < z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE(N)\\}$---relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} = \\min\\{\\delta : \\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)\\}$---exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity---confounding and mediation]\n\\label{thm:monotone}\nFor confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$ for some scalar $\\lambda$) under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\\delta \\in [0, \\delta_{\\max}]$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\n\\emph{Proof sketch.} Both topologies reduce to $B(C(\\delta)) {=} \\lambda V_W(\\delta)/(V_W(\\delta) {+} \\sigma^2)$ where $V_W(\\delta) {=} \\operatorname{Var}(g) {-} \\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g{\\mid}A^*_\\delta])$ and $\\sigma^2$ is a topology-specific constant ($\\sigma_T^2$ for confounding, $\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T{\\mid}A)]$ for mediation). As $\\delta$ increases, $A^*$ becomes a noisier channel for $A$, so $\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g{\\mid}A^*_\\delta])$ decreases by the data processing inequality, increasing $V_W$. Since $x/(x{+}c)$ is increasing for $c{>}0$, $|B|$ is non-decreasing (full proofs in Appendix~\\ref{app:monotonicity_proof} and~\\ref{par:med_monotone}). For general (non-proportional) effects, numerical verification across 10{,}000 random matrices confirms $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity with $\\varepsilon < 0.02$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:monotonicity_proof}).\n\n\\begin{conjecture}[$\\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]\n\\label{conj:monotone}\nFor the four remaining non-IV topologies (exposure, collider, M-bias, front-door) under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$: exposure and front-door are $\\varepsilon$-approximately non-decreasing in $\\delta$, and collider and M-bias (protective topologies) are $\\varepsilon$-approximately non-increasing in $\\delta$, with $\\varepsilon < 0.06$ ($<$6\\% of $\\tau$). Mediation envelope monotonicity is proved for proportional effects (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}) and verified numerically for the general case with $\\varepsilon < 0.02$. Verified across 10{,}000 random matrices per topology and $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ using analytic probability limits (Appendix~\\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}).\n\\end{conjecture}\n\nThe instrumental variable topology is excluded from Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}: the Wald estimator $\\hat{\\tau} = \\hat{\\gamma}_{Y{\\sim}A^*} / \\hat{\\gamma}_{T{\\sim}A^*}$ exhibits singularities when the first-stage coefficient passes through zero as $\\delta$ increases, producing unbounded deviations ($>10^6$). This is a property of the Wald estimator itself, not of the ASV framework; magnitude ASV remains well-defined for IV away from first-stage singularities.\n\nWhen strict monotonicity holds only approximately ($\\varepsilon < 0.06$), the reported ASV threshold $\\delta^*$ may differ from the true threshold by at most $\\varepsilon/\\eta$ in $\\delta$-units, where $\\eta$ is the practitioner's bias tolerance. At the recommended $\\eta = 10\\%$, this uncertainty is below 0.6 $\\delta$-steps---negligible for practical sensitivity assessment. Topology rankings (which topologies are most fragile) are preserved across all tested configurations.\n\nBinarization of $K{=}3$ annotations introduces additional topology-dependent bias (Appendix~\\ref{app:binarization}).\n",
